Scalarization for Multi-Task and\
Multi-Domain Learning at Scale<span class="ltx_pubnotes"><span class="ltx_pubnotes_content"><span class="ltx_pubnote ltx_role_thanks"><span class="ltx_note_name">Thanks: </span><span id="id2" class="ltx_text" style="font-size:70%;">    Qualcomm AI Research is an initiative of Qualcomm Technologies, Inc.</span></span></span></span>

<div id="abstract1" class="ltx_abstract">

###### Abstract

Training a single model on multiple input domains and/or output tasks
allows for compressing information from multiple sources into a unified
backbone hence improves model efficiency. It also enables potential
positive knowledge transfer across tasks/domains, leading to improved
accuracy and data-efficient training. However, optimizing such networks
is a challenge, in particular due to discrepancies between the different
tasks or domains: Despite several hypotheses and solutions proposed over
the years, recent work has shown that uniform scalarization training,
i.e., simply minimizing the average of the task losses, yields on-par
performance with more costly SotA optimization methods. This raises the
issue of how well we understand the training dynamics of multi-task and
multi-domain networks. In this work, we first devise a large-scale
unified analysis of multi-domain and multi-task learning to better
understand the dynamics of scalarization across varied task/domain
combinations and model sizes. Following these insights, we then propose
to leverage population-based training to efficiently search for the
optimal scalarization weights when dealing with a large number of tasks
or domains.

</div>

<div id="S1" class="section ltx_section">

## <span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_section">1 </span>Introduction

<div id="S1.p1" class="ltx_para">

Learning a unified architecture that can handle multiple domains and/or
tasks offers the potential for improved computational efficiency, better
generalization, and reduced training data requirements. However,
training such models proves a challenge: In particular, when the tasks
or domains are dissimilar, practitioners often observe that the learning
of one task or domain interferes with the learning of others. This
phenomenon is commonly known as interference or negative transfer. How
to best resolve this interference is an open problem spanning numerous
bodies of literature, such as multi-task learning, domain adaptation and
generalization, or multi-modal learning.

</div>

<div id="S1.p2" class="ltx_para">

In fact, there is currently no unanimous understanding of what causes,
or how to predict, task interference in practice: For instance,
classical generalization bounds on training from multiple data
sources \[<a href="#bib.bib3" class="ltx_ref">3</a>,
<a href="#bib.bib48" class="ltx_ref">48</a>,
<a href="#bib.bib56" class="ltx_ref">56</a>,
<a href="#bib.bib44" class="ltx_ref">44</a>\] involve a measure of
distance between the respective tasks/domains distributions, which can
be intuitively thought of as a measure of interference. In more
practical applications, task clustering
methods \[<a href="#bib.bib65" class="ltx_ref">65</a>,
<a href="#bib.bib58" class="ltx_ref">58</a>,
<a href="#bib.bib16" class="ltx_ref">16</a>\] propose various metrics to
measure task affinity, and train tasks with low affinity separately,
with separate backbones, thereby circumventing the interference issue at
the architecture level. Finally, in the multi-task optimization (MTO)
literature, the prevailing view is that gradients from different tasks
may point in conflicting directions and cause interference, which has
led to a line of work on reducing gradient conflicts by normalizing
gradients or losses
statistics \[<a href="#bib.bib37" class="ltx_ref">37</a>,
<a href="#bib.bib7" class="ltx_ref">7</a>,
<a href="#bib.bib25" class="ltx_ref">25</a>,
<a href="#bib.bib64" class="ltx_ref">64</a>\]. While MTO have
established a new state-of-the-art for training multi-task models in the
past few years, recent
work \[<a href="#bib.bib63" class="ltx_ref">63</a>,
<a href="#bib.bib30" class="ltx_ref">30</a>\] shows that, surprisingly,
minimizing a simple average of the tasks/domains losses, also known as
<span id="S1.p2.1.1"
class="ltx_text ltx_font_italic">scalarization</span>, can yield
performance trade-off points on the same Pareto front as more costly MTO
methods.

</div>

<div id="S1.p3" class="ltx_para">

Nevertheless, there still remains some unexplored areas which we aim to
shed lights on in this paper. First, most previous experiments have only
been conducted on multi-task settings, on benchmarks with either few
tasks or few training samples (e.g multi-MNIST, NYU-v2), and for a fixed
architecture. In particular, the link between model capacity and the
observed MTL performance is often overlooked. Secondly, it is not clear
how the choice of scalarization weights links to and/or impacts the
hypothesis of gradient conflicts as a key underlying signal of task
interference. Finally, scalarization can become prohibitively expensive
for a large number of tasks as the search space for the optimal loss
weights grows exponentially; This raises the issue of how to efficiently
browse this search space. In this work, we investigate these questions
to further motivate scalarization as a simple and scalable training
scheme for multi-task and multi-domain problems.

</div>

<div id="S1.p4" class="ltx_para">

<span id="S1.p4.1.1" class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold">Our key
contributions.</span> We perform a large-scale analysis of scalarization
for both multi-task (MTL) and multi-domain learning (MDL). We cover a
wide range of model capacities, datasets with varying sizes, and
different task/domain combinations. Our key conclusions are as follows:

</div>

<div id="S1.p5" class="ltx_para">

- <span id="S1.I1.i1"><span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_item">•</span></span>
  <div id="S1.I1.i1.p1" class="ltx_para">

  <span id="S1.I1.i1.p1.1.1" class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold">(C1)</span>
  When compared to a model trained on each task/domain individually,
  MDL/MTL performance tends to improve for larger model sizes, showing
  that the benefits of MTL/MDL frameworks should be put into perspective
  with respect to the backbone architecture capacity.

  </div>
- <span id="S1.I1.i2"><span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_item">•</span></span>
  <div id="S1.I1.i2.p1" class="ltx_para">

  <span id="S1.I1.i2.p1.1.1" class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold">(C2)</span>
  Tuning the scalarization weights for a specific tasks/domains
  combination is crucial to obtaining the optimal MTL/MDL performance in
  settings with high imbalance; Nevertheless, the relative performance
  of different scalarization weights is often consistent across model
  capacities inside a family architecture. This suggests that searching
  for optimal scalarization weights for a lower model depth/width is
  also relevant for the full model size, while using less compute.

  </div>
- <span id="S1.I1.i3"><span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_item">•</span></span>
  <div id="S1.I1.i3.p1" class="ltx_para">

  <span id="S1.I1.i3.p1.1.1" class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold">(C3)</span>
  Gradients conflicts between tasks/domains naturally occur during MTL
  training: They behave differently across layers and learning rates,
  but are scarcely impacted by model capacity and scalarization weights
  choice. These observations give new insights into the practical
  implications of avoiding conflicting gradients throughout training.

  </div>
- <span id="S1.I1.i4"><span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_item">•</span></span>
  <div id="S1.I1.i4.p1" class="ltx_para">

  <span id="S1.I1.i4.p1.1.1" class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold">(C4)</span>
  We leverage fast hyperparameter search methods such as
  population-based
  training \[<a href="#bib.bib24" class="ltx_ref">24</a>\] to
  efficiently browse the search space of scalarization weights as the
  number of tasks grows.

  </div>

</div>

</div>

<div id="S2" class="section ltx_section">

## <span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_section">2 </span>Related work

<div id="S2.SS0.SSS0.Px1" class="section ltx_paragraph">

#### Multi-Task Optimization (MTO) and scalarization.

<div id="S2.SS0.SSS0.Px1.p1" class="ltx_para">

MTO methods aim to improve MTL training by balancing the training
dynamics of different tasks. Prior research can be split into two
categories: On the one hand, loss-based methods propose to align the
task losses magnitudes by rescaling them through various criteria, e.g.,
task uncertainty \[<a href="#bib.bib26" class="ltx_ref">26</a>\], task
difficulty \[<a href="#bib.bib21" class="ltx_ref">21</a>\], random loss
weighting \[<a href="#bib.bib34" class="ltx_ref">34</a>\], or gradients
statistics \[<a href="#bib.bib7" class="ltx_ref">7</a>,
<a href="#bib.bib14" class="ltx_ref">14</a>,
<a href="#bib.bib60" class="ltx_ref">60</a>\]. On the other hand,
gradient-based methods directly act on the per-task gradients rather
than the losses. For instance,
\[<a href="#bib.bib55" class="ltx_ref">55</a>,
<a href="#bib.bib13" class="ltx_ref">13</a>\] tackle MTL as a
multi-objective optimization problem using the Multiple Gradient Descent
Algorithm to directly locate Pareto-optimal solutions. Another major
line of work considers conflicting gradient direction to be the main
cause of task interference. Consequently, these works
\[<a href="#bib.bib64" class="ltx_ref">64</a>,
<a href="#bib.bib37" class="ltx_ref">37</a>,
<a href="#bib.bib38" class="ltx_ref">38</a>,
<a href="#bib.bib62" class="ltx_ref">62</a>,
<a href="#bib.bib8" class="ltx_ref">8</a>,
<a href="#bib.bib25" class="ltx_ref">25</a>\] aim to mitigate conflicts
across per-task gradients. For instance, PCGrad
\[<a href="#bib.bib64" class="ltx_ref">64</a>\] suggests projecting each
task’s gradient onto the normal plane of other gradients to suppress
conflicting directions, while GradDrop
\[<a href="#bib.bib8" class="ltx_ref">8</a>\] ensures that all gradient
updates are pure in sign by randomly masking all positive or negative
gradient values during training. Unfortunately, gradient-based
techniques require per-task gradients, leading to substantial memory
usage and increased runtime. Furthermore, recent
work \[<a href="#bib.bib63" class="ltx_ref">63</a>,
<a href="#bib.bib30" class="ltx_ref">30</a>\] has shown that these
methods often perform on-par with the less costly approach of directly
optimizing the average of the task losses, also known as uniform
scalarization. Building off these insights, we further investigate the
benefits of scalarization and the practical dynamics of gradient
conflicts in this work.

</div>

</div>

<div id="S2.SS0.SSS0.Px2" class="section ltx_paragraph">

#### Architectures for MTL.

<div id="S2.SS0.SSS0.Px2.p1" class="ltx_para">

Orthogonal to our work, another line of research focuses on designing
multi-task architectures that mitigate task interference by optimizing
the allocation of shared versus task-specific parameters. Hard parameter
sharing methods \[<a href="#bib.bib27" class="ltx_ref">27</a>,
<a href="#bib.bib26" class="ltx_ref">26</a>,
<a href="#bib.bib6" class="ltx_ref">6</a>,
<a href="#bib.bib59" class="ltx_ref">59</a>,
<a href="#bib.bib61" class="ltx_ref">61</a>\] build off a shared
backbone and carefully design task-specific decoder heads optimized for
the tasks of interest. In contrast, soft parameter sharing
methods \[<a href="#bib.bib45" class="ltx_ref">45</a>,
<a href="#bib.bib53" class="ltx_ref">53</a>,
<a href="#bib.bib20" class="ltx_ref">20</a>,
<a href="#bib.bib39" class="ltx_ref">39</a>\] jointly train a shared
backbone while learning sparse binary masks specific to each task/domain
which capture the parameter sharing pattern across tasks. To select
which tasks should share parameters or not, a prominent line of work
focuses on defining measures of task
affinity \[<a href="#bib.bib2" class="ltx_ref">2</a>,
<a href="#bib.bib58" class="ltx_ref">58</a>,
<a href="#bib.bib22" class="ltx_ref">22</a>,
<a href="#bib.bib16" class="ltx_ref">16</a>\]: Tasks with high affinity
are jointly trained, while low affinity ones use different backbones, in
the hope of reducing potential interference by fully separating
conflicting tasks. This results in multi-branch architectures where each
branch handles a specific subset of tasks with high affinities. The
scope of our study is complementary to this line of work, as we focus on
how to best train a given set of tasks, without changing the
architecture.

</div>

</div>

<div id="S2.SS0.SSS0.Px3" class="section ltx_paragraph">

#### Multi-Domain Learning (MDL).

<div id="S2.SS0.SSS0.Px3.p1" class="ltx_para">

MDL refers to the design and training of a single unified architecture
that can operate on data sources from different domains. Domain
adaptation and generalization methods often aim to align the learned
representations of the different
domains \[<a href="#bib.bib19" class="ltx_ref">19</a>,
<a href="#bib.bib4" class="ltx_ref">4</a>,
<a href="#bib.bib66" class="ltx_ref">66</a>\]; These are in particular
targeting the setting where one of the domain lacks supervisory signals.
In the fully-supervised scenario, several methods have been proposed to
use a unified backbone, which captures common features across all tasks,
while allowing the model to learn domain-specific information via
lightweight adapter modules
\[<a href="#bib.bib49" class="ltx_ref">49</a>,
<a href="#bib.bib51" class="ltx_ref">51</a>,
<a href="#bib.bib50" class="ltx_ref">50</a>\]. Similar to soft parameter
sharing method, these methods bypass task interference by keeping the
shared backbone frozen and only training domain-specific modules.
Alternatively, a common practice when training with multiple input
datasets is to use over- or undersampling
techniques \[<a href="#bib.bib1" class="ltx_ref">1</a>,
<a href="#bib.bib28" class="ltx_ref">28</a>,
<a href="#bib.bib43" class="ltx_ref">43</a>\], in particular to handle
class imbalance. As we describe later, resampling can be seen as the MDL
counterpart of scalarization in MTL. Both enable a simple and
lightweight training scheme, yet finding the optimal
resampling/scalarizatoin weights is nontrivial. This motivates us to
empirically verify our findings in the MDL as well as MTL setting.

</div>

</div>

</div>

<div id="S3" class="section ltx_section">

## <span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_section">3 </span>Motivation and experimental setting for MTL/MDL

<div id="S3.SS1" class="section ltx_subsection">

### <span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_subsection">3.1 </span>Notations

<div id="S3.SS1.p1" class="ltx_para">

We start by describing the MTL/MDL setup: Given $`T`$ supervised
datasets with respective inputs $`X_{t}`$ and outputs $`Y_{t}`$, our
goal is to find model parameters $`\theta^{\ast}`$ that minimize the
datasets’ respective losses:
$`(\mathcal{L}_{1}(X_{1},Y_{1}),\dots,\mathcal{L}_{T}(X_{T},Y_{T}))`$.
In practice, this can be achieved either by solving a multi-objective
problem on the task
losses \[<a href="#bib.bib13" class="ltx_ref">13</a>,
<a href="#bib.bib42" class="ltx_ref">42</a>\] or, more commonly, by
minimizing a (possibly weighted) average of the
losses \[<a href="#bib.bib27" class="ltx_ref">27</a>\]. In both cases,
most training methods for MTL/MDL can be phrased as updating the model
parameters $`\theta`$ using a weighted average of the individual tasks’
gradients:

|  |  |  |  |  |
|----|----|----|----|----|
|  | $`\displaystyle\theta_{i+1}`$ | $`\displaystyle=\theta_{i}+\eta\sum_{t=1}^{T}p_{t}^{i}\ \mathbb{E}_{(x_{t},y_{t})\sim q(X_{t},Y_{t})}\nabla_{\theta_{i}}\mathcal{L}_{t}(x_{t},y_{t})\text{ , where }\eta\text{ is the learning rate}`$ |  | <span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_equation ltx_align_right">(1)</span> |

where $`\mathbf{p(i)}=(p_{1}^{i},\dots,p_{T}^{i})`$ captures the dynamic
importance scaling weights for each dataset at timestep $`i`$, and
$`q(X_{t},Y_{t})`$ is the underlying distribution the $`t`$-th dataset
is drawn from. We also follow the common assumption that $`\mathbf{p}`$
is a probability distribution, <span id="S3.SS1.p1.2.1"
class="ltx_text ltx_font_italic">i.e.</span>, $`\sum_{t}p_{t}=1`$ and
$`\forall t,\ p_{t}\in\{0,1\}`$.

</div>

<div id="S3.SS1.p2" class="ltx_para">

We further distinguish between <span id="S3.SS1.p2.1.1"
class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold">(i)</span> multi-task learning (MTL)
where every input is annotated for every task (i.e.,
$`\forall i,j,X_{i}=X_{j}`$), and <span id="S3.SS1.p2.1.2"
class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold">(ii)</span> multi-domain learning (MDL)
where the goal is to solve a common output task across multiple input
domains or modalities ($`\forall i,j,Y_{i}=Y_{j}`$). In the MDL setting,
we can also rephrase <a href="#S3.E1" class="ltx_ref"
title="In 3.1 Notations ‣ 3 Motivation and experimental setting for MTL/MDL ‣ Scalarization for Multi-Task and Multi-Domain Learning at Scale">(<span
class="ltx_ref"><span class="ltx_text ltx_ref_tag">1</span></span>)</a>
as resampling the data distributions according to $`\mathbf{p}`$. Both
formalisms are equivalent, but resampling often performs better in
practice when using stochastic gradient
methods \[<a href="#bib.bib1" class="ltx_ref">1</a>\]:

|  |  |  |  |
|----|----|----|----|
|  | $`\displaystyle\underbrace{\sum_{t=1}^{T}p_{t}\mathbb{E}_{q(X_{t},Y_{t})}f(x_{t},y_{t})}_{\text{Reweighing formalism of \eqref{mtleq}}}=\underbrace{\vphantom{\sum_{t=1}^{T}p_{t}}\mathbb{E}_{q^{\prime}(X,Y)}f(x,y)}_{\text{Resampling formalism}}\text{ where }\underbrace{\vphantom{\sum_{t=1}^{T}p_{t}}q^{\prime}(x,y)=\sum_{t=1}^{T}\mathbbm{1}_{x\in X_{t}}q(x,y)p_{t}}_{\text{Resampling distribution}}`$ |  | <span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_equation ltx_align_right">(2)</span> |

</div>

</div>

<div id="S3.SS2" class="section ltx_subsection">

### <span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_subsection">3.2 </span>Motivation

<div id="S3.SS2.p1" class="ltx_para">

Current state-of-the-art methods for training objectives such as
<a href="#S3.E1" class="ltx_ref"
title="In 3.1 Notations ‣ 3 Motivation and experimental setting for MTL/MDL ‣ Scalarization for Multi-Task and Multi-Domain Learning at Scale">(<span
class="ltx_ref"><span class="ltx_text ltx_ref_tag">1</span></span>)</a>
can be roughly organized into three categories, based on how the
datasets’ importance weights $`\mathbf{p}`$ are defined:

- <span id="S3.I1.i1"><span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_item">•</span></span>
  <div id="S3.I1.i1.p1" class="ltx_para">

  <span id="S3.I1.i1.p1.1.1"
  class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold">Scalarization</span> defines the
  weights $`p_{t}`$ as constant hyperparameters: The update rule of
  <a href="#S3.E1" class="ltx_ref"
  title="In 3.1 Notations ‣ 3 Motivation and experimental setting for MTL/MDL ‣ Scalarization for Multi-Task and Multi-Domain Learning at Scale">(<span
  class="ltx_ref"><span class="ltx_text ltx_ref_tag">1</span></span>)</a>
  reduces to computing the gradient of the weighted average loss
  $`\nabla_{\theta}\left(\sum_{t}p_{t}\mathcal{L}_{t}\right)`$. In the
  MDL formalism of (<a href="#S3.E2" class="ltx_ref"
  title="In 3.1 Notations ‣ 3 Motivation and experimental setting for MTL/MDL ‣ Scalarization for Multi-Task and Multi-Domain Learning at Scale"><span
  class="ltx_text ltx_ref_tag">2</span></a>), scalarization is similar
  to classical oversampling/undersampling, where $`p_{t}`$ is defined
  either as a scalar (per-domain) or a vector (to handle both per-class
  and per-domain biases). In both settings, the key difficulty lies in
  tuning the weights $`\mathbf{p}`$, as the search space grows
  exponentially with $`T`$.

  </div>
- <span id="S3.I1.i2"><span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_item">•</span></span>
  <div id="S3.I1.i2.p1" class="ltx_para">

  <span id="S3.I1.i2.p1.1.1" class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold">Loss-based
  adaptive methods</span> \[<a href="#bib.bib38" class="ltx_ref">38</a>,
  <a href="#bib.bib26" class="ltx_ref">26</a>\] dynamically compute
  $`p_{t}`$ for every batch, aiming to uniformize training dynamics
  across tasks by rescaling the losses. For instance, IMTL-L
  \[<a href="#bib.bib38" class="ltx_ref">38</a>\] dynamically reweighs
  the losses such that they all have the same magnitude.

  </div>
- <span id="S3.I1.i3"><span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_item">•</span></span>
  <div id="S3.I1.i3.p1" class="ltx_para">

  <span id="S3.I1.i3.p1.1.1"
  class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold">Gradient-based adaptive
  methods</span> \[<a href="#bib.bib7" class="ltx_ref">7</a>,
  <a href="#bib.bib64" class="ltx_ref">64</a>,
  <a href="#bib.bib38" class="ltx_ref">38</a>,
  <a href="#bib.bib25" class="ltx_ref">25</a>,
  <a href="#bib.bib8" class="ltx_ref">8</a>,
  <a href="#bib.bib37" class="ltx_ref">37</a>,
  <a href="#bib.bib13" class="ltx_ref">13</a>,
  <a href="#bib.bib55" class="ltx_ref">55</a>,
  <a href="#bib.bib36" class="ltx_ref">36</a>,
  <a href="#bib.bib42" class="ltx_ref">42</a>\] also dynamically compute
  weights for every batch. While this line of work usually outperforms
  loss-based methods, they also incur a higher compute and memory
  training cost, as they require $`T`$ backward passes to obtain each
  individual dataset gradient, which in turn need to be stored in
  memory.

  </div>

</div>

<div id="S3.SS2.p2" class="ltx_para">

While gradient-based approaches are generally considered SotA across MTO
methods, they present certain practical challenges: They come with a
higher computational and memory cost, which increases with $`T`$, as
illustrated in <a href="#S3.F1" class="ltx_ref"
title="Figure 1 ‣ 3.2 Motivation ‣ 3 Motivation and experimental setting for MTL/MDL ‣ Scalarization for Multi-Task and Multi-Domain Learning at Scale">Figure
<span class="ltx_ref"><span
class="ltx_text ltx_ref_tag">1</span></span>(b)</a>. The implicit
weights $`\mathbf{p}_{t}`$ computed by these methods are also hard to
extract and transfer to other training runs since they are tied to other
hyperparameters (training length, batch size, gradient clipping, etc.).
In addition, recent work \[<a href="#bib.bib63" class="ltx_ref">63</a>,
<a href="#bib.bib30" class="ltx_ref">30</a>,
<a href="#bib.bib52" class="ltx_ref">52</a>\] has shown that simple
scalarization with uniform weights actually often performs on-par with
both loss- and gradient-based methods. Furthermore, scalarization is
highly practical: It does not incur additional costs during training,
and scalarization weights are easily interpretable as a measure of “task
importance” in the optimization problem. However, some experiments in
\[<a href="#bib.bib58" class="ltx_ref">58</a>,
<a href="#bib.bib52" class="ltx_ref">52</a>\] also suggest that the
benefits of uniform scalarization MTL are impacted by model capacity in
some settings.

</div>

<div id="S3.SS2.p3" class="ltx_para">

Motivated by these insights, we aim to better harness scalarization for
scalable and practical MTL/MDL training. In particular, we focus on the
following two remaining gray areas: <span id="S3.SS2.p3.1.1"
class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold">First</span>, we perform a large-scale
analysis investigating the underlying effects of model capacity on MTL
and MDL <span id="S3.SS2.p3.1.2"
class="ltx_text ltx_font_italic">(C1)</span> in
<a href="#S4" class="ltx_ref"
title="4 Benefits of MDL/MTL under the lens of model capacity ‣ Scalarization for Multi-Task and Multi-Domain Learning at Scale">Section
<span class="ltx_ref"><span
class="ltx_text ltx_ref_tag">4</span></span></a>. We then discuss the
impact of model capacity on the choice of optimal scalarization weights
<span id="S3.SS2.p3.1.3" class="ltx_text ltx_font_italic">(C2)</span> in
<a href="#S4.SS1" class="ltx_ref"
title="4.1 Selecting optimal scalarization weights 𝐩^∗ ‣ 4 Benefits of MDL/MTL under the lens of model capacity ‣ Scalarization for Multi-Task and Multi-Domain Learning at Scale">Section
<span class="ltx_ref"><span
class="ltx_text ltx_ref_tag">4.1</span></span></a> and on the presence
of conflicting gradients <span id="S3.SS2.p3.1.4"
class="ltx_text ltx_font_italic">(C3)</span> in
<a href="#S4.SS2" class="ltx_ref"
title="4.2 Conflicting gradients in practice ‣ 4 Benefits of MDL/MTL under the lens of model capacity ‣ Scalarization for Multi-Task and Multi-Domain Learning at Scale">Section
<span class="ltx_ref"><span
class="ltx_text ltx_ref_tag">4.2</span></span></a>.
<span id="S3.SS2.p3.1.5" class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold">Secondly</span>,
browsing the search space of $`\mathbf{p}`$ to tune the scalarization
weights becomes increasingly costly as the number of tasks $`T`$ grows.
As an alternative to the usual grid- or random-search approaches, we
propose to leverage population-based training as a cost-efficient
parameter search for scalarization, and report our conclusions
<span id="S3.SS2.p3.1.6" class="ltx_text ltx_font_italic">(C4)</span> in
<a href="#S5" class="ltx_ref"
title="5 Population-based training for scalarization weights selection ‣ Scalarization for Multi-Task and Multi-Domain Learning at Scale">Section
<span class="ltx_ref"><span
class="ltx_text ltx_ref_tag">5</span></span></a>.

</div>

<p><span id="S3.F1.2.2.1" class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold">(a)</span>
Summary of our experimental setting</p>
</div>
</div>
<div class="ltx_flex_cell ltx_flex_size_2">
<div id="S3.F1.3"
class="ltx_block ltx_figure_panel ltx_minipage ltx_align_center ltx_align_bottom"
style="width:127.6pt;">
<p><span id="S3.F1.3.1.1" class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold">(b)</span>
<span id="S3.F1.3.1.2" class="ltx_text" style="font-size:90%;">Memory
usage of various multi-task optimization (MTO) methods</span></p>
</div>
</div>
</div>
<figcaption><span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_figure">Figure 1: </span> Table
<span id="S3.F1.6" class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold">(a)</span> summarizes
the experimental setups used throughout the paper. Figure <span
id="S3.F1.7" class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold">(b)</span> reports profiling
results for popular multi-task optimization (MTO) methods in a
small-scale (ResNet18, batch size 16) and large-scale (ResNet50, size
128) setting, for 224px inputs: In practice, we find that the main
bottleneck for gradient-based methods is their high memory usage.
Consequently, training with reasonable batch sizes requires either high
parallelism (compute demand) or gradient accumulation (slower runs). In
contrast, loss-based methods have much better-constrained costs.
However, they usually underperform their gradient-based counterpart in
the literature.</figcaption>
</figure>

</div>

<div id="S3.SS3" class="section ltx_subsection">

### <span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_subsection">3.3 </span>Experimental setting

<div id="S3.SS3.p1" class="ltx_para">

Here we briefly describe the experimental setup that is used throughout
the paper. We aim to cover a wide range of model sizes, datasets with
varying sizes, and different task/domain combinations for MDL and MTL. A
summary is given in <a href="#S3.F1" class="ltx_ref"
title="Figure 1 ‣ 3.2 Motivation ‣ 3 Motivation and experimental setting for MTL/MDL ‣ Scalarization for Multi-Task and Multi-Domain Learning at Scale">Figure
<span class="ltx_ref"><span
class="ltx_text ltx_ref_tag">1</span></span>(a)</a> and further details
can be found in <a href="#S1a" class="ltx_ref"
title="1 Experimental settings and training hyperparameters ‣ Scalarization for Multi-Task and Multi-Domain Learning at Scale">Appendix
<span class="ltx_ref"><span
class="ltx_text ltx_ref_tag">1</span></span></a>.

</div>

<div id="S3.SS3.p2" class="ltx_para">

For MDL, we first consider a two-domain example composed of
CIFAR10 \[<a href="#bib.bib29" class="ltx_ref">29</a>\] and
STL10 \[<a href="#bib.bib9" class="ltx_ref">9</a>\] with a ViT-S
backbone optimized for small
datasets \[<a href="#bib.bib18" class="ltx_ref">18</a>\]. We then expand
the results to the DomainNet
benchmark \[<a href="#bib.bib47" class="ltx_ref">47</a>\] containing 345
output classes and 6 input domains. We use ResNet as our main backbone,
following previous works \[<a href="#bib.bib47" class="ltx_ref">47</a>,
<a href="#bib.bib32" class="ltx_ref">32</a>\]. In both cases, the
backbone parameters are fully shared across all domains; We use
cross-entropy as training loss and top-1 accuracy as our main metric.

</div>

<div id="S3.SS3.p3" class="ltx_para">

For MTL, we use CelebA \[<a href="#bib.bib40" class="ltx_ref">40</a>\]
with the same ViT-S backbone as for CIFAR/STL; We split the 40
attributes into semantically-coherent groups to form 8 output
classification tasks, as described in the appendix. The transformer
backbone is fully shared across tasks, while the last linear
classification layer is task-specific. Then, we experiment on a larger
MTL setting for dense prediction: Most traditional benchmarks, such as
Cityscapes \[<a href="#bib.bib10" class="ltx_ref">10</a>\] and
NYU-v2 \[<a href="#bib.bib57" class="ltx_ref">57</a>\], are rather
small, as they only contain 2-3 dense tasks (segmentation, normals,
depth) and $`\sim`$ 5k fully annotated images. Instead, we perform our
analysis on the challenging Taskonomy
dataset \[<a href="#bib.bib65" class="ltx_ref">65</a>\]. To keep
experiments scalable, we filter the 26 tasks in Taskonomy down to 7
(e.g. by removing self-supervised tasks or clearly overlapping ones such
as 2D edges and 3D edges). For training, we follow the setup described
in \[<a href="#bib.bib58" class="ltx_ref">58</a>\]: We normalize every
dense annotation to have zero mean and unit variance across the training
set, and use the $`L_{1}`$ loss as our training loss and common metric
for all tasks. The backbone is composed of a (shared) ResNet encoder,
followed by task-specific decoders built with upsampling operations and
1x1 convolutions. To control model capacity, we vary
<span id="S3.SS3.p3.1.1" class="ltx_text ltx_font_italic">(i)</span> the
width of the model, <span id="S3.SS3.p3.1.2"
class="ltx_text ltx_font_italic">(ii)</span> the depth of the encoder,
as well as <span id="S3.SS3.p3.1.3"
class="ltx_text ltx_font_italic">(iii)</span> the number of layers in
the decoder(s) which are shared across tasks.

</div>

<div id="S3.SS3.p4" class="ltx_para">

Finally, in all settings, we sweep over the domains/tasks’ weights under
the common assumption that $`p`$ is a probability vector (i.e.
$`\forall t,\ p_{t}\in\{0,1\}\text{ and }\sum_{t}p_{t}=1`$). All
reported results are averaged across 2 random seeds for DomainNet and
Taskonomy, and 3 for the smaller benchmarks. Unless stated, every
experiment is conducted on a single NVIDIA V100 GPU.

</div>

</div>

</div>

<div id="S4" class="section ltx_section">

## <span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_section">4 </span>Benefits of MDL/MTL under the lens of model capacity

<div id="S4.p1" class="ltx_para">

We first investigate the behavior of scalarization for MDL/MTL training
with two tasks, while varying model capacity and for different
tasks/domains combinations. To quantify the benefit or harm of joint
training with multiple datasets, we compare the model performance on
each dataset with that of a same-sized model, trained on a single
dataset at once. We refer to this baseline as “SD” (single dataset).
Since we use SD as a reference point, we ensure that its training
pipeline is well tuned: We sweep over the hyperparameters (learning
rate, weight decay, number of training steps, etc) of the baseline SD
and keep the same values for training the MDL/MTL models, while varying
the task weights $`(p_{1},p_{2}=1-p_{1})`$ from $`p_{1}=0`$ (SD baseline
of the first dataset) to $`p_{1}=1`$ (SD baseline of the second
dataset). Further details on the training hyperparameters can be found
in <a href="#S1a" class="ltx_ref"
title="1 Experimental settings and training hyperparameters ‣ Scalarization for Multi-Task and Multi-Domain Learning at Scale">Appendix
<span class="ltx_ref"><span
class="ltx_text ltx_ref_tag">1</span></span></a>. Finally, we summarize
our analysis results in <a href="#S4.F2" class="ltx_ref"
title="Figure 2 ‣ 4 Benefits of MDL/MTL under the lens of model capacity ‣ Scalarization for Multi-Task and Multi-Domain Learning at Scale">Figure
<span class="ltx_ref"><span
class="ltx_text ltx_ref_tag">2</span></span></a>: For each task/domain
pair, we first plot the weight $`p^{\ast}_{1}`$ yielding the best
accuracy averaged across both tasks/domains, then we report the accuracy
difference between the corresponding MDL/MTL model and the associated SD
baseline.

</div>

<div id="S4.p2" class="ltx_para">

<span id="S4.p2.1.1" class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold">Impact of model
capacity.</span> We first observe that MDL/MTL performance greatly
varies across model capacities, and tends to increase with model size
(<span id="S4.p2.1.2" class="ltx_text ltx_font_italic">C1</span>): In
some cases, the trend even inverts from MDL/MTL underperforming to
outperforming SD (e.g. DomainNet’s real + sketch pairing).

</div>

<div id="S4.p3" class="ltx_para">

<span id="S4.p3.1.1" class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold">Selecting optimal
importance weights $`\mathbf{p}`$</span>. Secondly, the best-performing
$`\mathbf{p}`$ at inference are rarely the uniform $`p_{1}=p_{2}=0.5`$,
even in the Taskonomy scenario where the uniform training objective is
identical to the average of task metrics which we aim to maximize at
inference. We further discuss the effects of tuning scalarization
weights for the accuracy/efficiency trade-off <span id="S4.p3.1.2"
class="ltx_text ltx_font_italic">(C2)</span> in
<a href="#S4.SS1" class="ltx_ref"
title="4.1 Selecting optimal scalarization weights 𝐩^∗ ‣ 4 Benefits of MDL/MTL under the lens of model capacity ‣ Scalarization for Multi-Task and Multi-Domain Learning at Scale">Section
<span class="ltx_ref"><span
class="ltx_text ltx_ref_tag">4.1</span></span></a>.

</div>

<div id="S4.p4" class="ltx_para">

<span id="S4.p4.1.1" class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold">MTL/MDL primarily
improves generalization.</span> As we show in
<a href="#S2a" class="ltx_ref"
title="2 Additional analysis results ‣ Scalarization for Multi-Task and Multi-Domain Learning at Scale">Appendix
<span class="ltx_ref"><span
class="ltx_text ltx_ref_tag">2</span></span></a>), even when MTL/MDL
outperforms the SD baseline at inference, it is generally not the case
at training time. In other words, tuning the respective tasks/domains
weights in MTL/MDL can be seen as a regularization technique that
improves generalization over the SD baselines by balancing the two
datasets’ training objectives. In particular, in the MDL setting, this
insight is also closely connected to data augmentations: Each new domain
can be seen as a non-parametric and non-trivial augmentation function,
while the associated resampling weight $`p_{t}`$ is the probability of
applying the augmentation on each sample.

</div>

<div id="S4.p5" class="ltx_para">

Interestingly, recent
work \[<a href="#bib.bib30" class="ltx_ref">30</a>\] suggests that many
MTO methods which aim to avoid gradient conflicts can also be
interpreted as regularization techniques, and that simpler tricks such
as early stopping are often competitive. To better understand the link
between these two insights, we analyze the natural emergence of
conflicting gradients during MDL/MTL training, and how they are impacted
by model capacity <span id="S4.p5.1.1"
class="ltx_text ltx_font_italic">(C3)</span> in
<a href="#S4.SS2" class="ltx_ref"
title="4.2 Conflicting gradients in practice ‣ 4 Benefits of MDL/MTL under the lens of model capacity ‣ Scalarization for Multi-Task and Multi-Domain Learning at Scale">Section
<span class="ltx_ref"><span
class="ltx_text ltx_ref_tag">4.2</span></span></a>.

</div>

<figure id="S4.F2" class="ltx_figure">
<div class="ltx_flex_figure">
<div class="ltx_flex_cell ltx_flex_size_1">
<div id="S4.F2.2"
class="ltx_block ltx_figure_panel ltx_minipage ltx_align_center ltx_align_middle"
style="width:345.0pt;">

STL-10 toy example (right)</span></span></p>

</div>

</div>

<figcaption><span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_figure">Figure 2:
</span>Performance of scalarization for MDL/MTL relative to SD under
different model capacities; Each column corresponds to a different
task/domain pair
(<math display="inline" xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><semantics><mrow><mi>T</mi><mo>=</mo><mn>2</mn></mrow><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">T=2</annotation></semantics></math>).
The first row of each plot contains a heatmap of the best performing
scalarization weights
<math display="inline" xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><semantics><msup><mi>p</mi><mo>*</mo></msup><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">p^{\ast}</annotation></semantics></math>
wrt. to the average test accuracy on both tasks. Each of the following
rows contains the difference in metrics between the MTL/MDL model and
its counterpart SD baseline, where green indicates positive changes and
purple, a negative one. Note that the colormaps’ ranges are defined
<span id="S4.F2.5" class="ltx_text ltx_font_italic">per row</span>, and
visualized as color bars at the beginning of each row. We observe the
general trend that the performance improvement of MDL, relative to the
corresponding SD baseline, tends to increase with model
capacity.</figcaption>
</figure>

<div id="S4.SS1" class="section ltx_subsection">

### <span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_subsection">4.1 </span>Selecting optimal scalarization weights $`\mathbf{p}^{\ast}`$

<div id="S4.SS1.p1" class="ltx_para">

So far, we have discussed MDL/MTL improvement over the SD baseline when
selecting the weights $`p^{\ast}`$ that maximize average accuracy.
However, in real-world settings, many criteria come into play when
evaluating MTL/MDL models: For instance, one task may be more critical
than others hence the model should be evaluated via weighted accuracy.
Second, even if the MTL/MDL model underperforms the corresponding SD
baseline at the same model capacity, it may still improve the
efficiency-to-accuracy trade-off when both tasks need to be solved at
once. To give a clearer overview of how these considerations affect the
choice of weights $`\mathbf{p}`$, we report results for all
scalarization weights in <a href="#S4.F3" class="ltx_ref"
title="Figure 3 ‣ 4.1 Selecting optimal scalarization weights 𝐩^∗ ‣ 4 Benefits of MDL/MTL under the lens of model capacity ‣ Scalarization for Multi-Task and Multi-Domain Learning at Scale">Figure
<span class="ltx_ref"><span
class="ltx_text ltx_ref_tag">3</span></span></a>; We also highlight
points where MTL/MDL outperforms SD on <span id="S4.SS1.p1.1.1"
class="ltx_text ltx_font_italic">both</span> domains/tasks, and
represent each model’s number of parameters as the marker size.

</div>

<div id="S4.SS1.p2" class="ltx_para">

First, we observe a clear asymmetry in terms of performance across the
$`T=2`$ datasets, even when using uniform weighing $`p_{1}=0.5`$: For
instance on Taskonomy (<a href="#S4.F3" class="ltx_ref"
title="Figure 3 ‣ 4.1 Selecting optimal scalarization weights 𝐩^∗ ‣ 4 Benefits of MDL/MTL under the lens of model capacity ‣ Scalarization for Multi-Task and Multi-Domain Learning at Scale">Figure
<span class="ltx_ref"><span
class="ltx_text ltx_ref_tag">3</span></span></a>), several MTL models
outperform SD on the depth prediction task, but none on the semantic
segmentation task. Nevertheless, MTL models are very appealing when
taking model efficiency into account, as they contain roughly half as
many parameters. Secondly, tuning the scalarization weights is crucial
in some settings: For instance in DomainNet, training with
$`p_{1}\in[0.65,0.75]`$ is more advantageous than uniform scalarization.
Furthermore, the relative ranking of the weight $`p_{1}`$, with respect
to model performance, does not change significantly across model
capacities, for both the Taskonomy and DomainNet examples. This suggests
that optimal weights $`\mathbf{p}^{\ast}`$ for a given model may be a
good search starting point for another architecture of the same family
(see <a href="#S4a" class="ltx_ref"
title="4 Consistency of optimal scalarization weights ‣ Scalarization for Multi-Task and Multi-Domain Learning at Scale">Appendix
<span class="ltx_ref"><span
class="ltx_text ltx_ref_tag">4</span></span></a>).

</div>

<div id="S4.SS1.p3" class="ltx_para">

Nevertheless, the search space for $`\mathbf{p}`$ grows exponentially
with $`T`$, making the search computationally prohibitive, even for one
architecture. In <a href="#S5" class="ltx_ref"
title="5 Population-based training for scalarization weights selection ‣ Scalarization for Multi-Task and Multi-Domain Learning at Scale">Section
<span class="ltx_ref"><span
class="ltx_text ltx_ref_tag">5</span></span></a>, we propose a scalable
approach to optimize scalarization weights and investigate its
performance on DomainNet and CelebA.

</div>

<figure id="S4.F3" class="ltx_figure">
<div class="ltx_flex_figure">
<div class="ltx_flex_cell ltx_flex_size_2">
<div id="S4.F3.2"
class="ltx_block ltx_figure_panel ltx_minipage ltx_align_center ltx_align_middle"
style="width:165.6pt;">
<p><span id="S4.F3.2.1.1" class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold"
style="font-size:80%;">(a)<span id="S4.F3.2.1.1.1"
class="ltx_text ltx_font_medium"> DomainNet - real + painting (<span
id="S4.F3.2.1.1.1.1" class="ltx_text ltx_font_italic">higher is
better</span>)</span></span></p>
</div>
</div>

class="ltx_block ltx_figure_panel ltx_minipage ltx_align_center ltx_align_middle"

MDL/MTL performance when varying tasks weight
<math display="inline" xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><semantics><msub><mi>p</mi><mn>1</mn></msub><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">p_{1}</annotation></semantics></math>

</div>

<div id="S4.SS2" class="section ltx_subsection">

### <span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_subsection">4.2 </span>Conflicting gradients in practice

<div id="S4.SS2.p1" class="ltx_para">

A widely spread explanation for task interference in the literature is
that individual task gradients may point in conflicting directions,
hampering training. To investigate this behavior, we measure the
percentage of conflicting gradients pair encountered in each epoch, when
training with uniform scalarization. Following
\[<a href="#bib.bib64" class="ltx_ref">64</a>\], we define gradients as
conflicting if and only if the cosine of their angle is negative.
Finally, we provide further details and figures for this section in
<a href="#S3a" class="ltx_ref"
title="3 Methodology for measuring gradients conflicts ‣ Scalarization for Multi-Task and Multi-Domain Learning at Scale">Appendix
<span class="ltx_ref"><span
class="ltx_text ltx_ref_tag">3</span></span></a>.

</div>

<div id="S4.SS2.p2" class="ltx_para">

In <a href="#S4.F4" class="ltx_ref"
title="Figure 4 ‣ 4.2 Conflicting gradients in practice ‣ 4 Benefits of MDL/MTL under the lens of model capacity ‣ Scalarization for Multi-Task and Multi-Domain Learning at Scale">Figure
<span class="ltx_ref"><span
class="ltx_text ltx_ref_tag">4</span></span></a>, we first illustrate an
asymmetric characteristic of gradient conflicts: A high number of
gradient conflicts typically translates to poorly performing models
(e.g. early training), but the lower conflicts regime does not correlate
well with MDL/MTL performance. In particular, it is common to encounter
more gradient conflicts towards the end of training, while the loss
steadily decreases. This suggests that, in practice, identifying and
removing conflicts at every training iteration may be superfluous, with
respect to the compute and memory cost occurred. We can also put this
observation in perspective with Theorem 3 of
\[<a href="#bib.bib64" class="ltx_ref">64</a>\], which states that in
the case of two tasks, a parameter update of PCGrad leads to a lower
loss than the uniform scalarization update if the tasks are conflicting
enough; however, this assumption may not hold true across every training
iteration.

</div>

<figure id="S4.F4" class="ltx_figure">
<div class="ltx_flex_figure">
<div class="ltx_flex_cell ltx_flex_size_1">

</div>
<div class="ltx_flex_break">

</div>
<div class="ltx_flex_cell ltx_flex_size_1">
<p><span id="S4.F4.2.1" class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold"
style="font-size:90%;">(a)<span id="S4.F4.2.1.1"
class="ltx_text ltx_font_medium"> Per-epoch proportion of gradient
conflicts during training</span></span></p>
</div>
<div class="ltx_flex_break">

</div>
<div class="ltx_flex_cell ltx_flex_size_1">
<p><span id="S4.F4.3.1" class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold"
style="font-size:90%;">(b)<span id="S4.F4.3.1.1"
class="ltx_text ltx_font_medium"> Correlation plot with per-epoch
average training loss</span></span></p>
</div>
<div class="ltx_flex_break">

</div>
<div class="ltx_flex_cell ltx_flex_size_1">
<p><span id="S4.F4.4.1" class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold"
style="font-size:90%;">(c)<span id="S4.F4.4.1.1"
class="ltx_text ltx_font_medium"> Correlation plot with per-epoch test
metric</span></span></p>
</div>
</div>
<figcaption><span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_figure">Figure 4: </span>
Proportion of conflicting gradients during uniform scalarization
training <span id="S4.F4.7" class="ltx_text"
style="--ltx-fg-color:#0000FF;">with</span> and <span id="S4.F4.8"
class="ltx_text" style="--ltx-fg-color:#FF8000;">without</span> PCGrad
on the 40 attribute classification tasks of CelebA while varying
learning rate ([5e-4, 5e-3, 1e-2]), model depth ([3, 6, 9]) and width
([0.5, 0.75, 1]). While the overall number of encountered conflicts
differs, the trend is consistent across both settings in that the higher
number of conflicts encountered towards the end of training does not
harm training or final model performance.</figcaption>
</figure>

<div id="S4.SS2.p3" class="ltx_para">

Secondly, we analyze the effect of different factors of training
variations on the observed proportion of gradient conflicts in
<a href="#S4.F5" class="ltx_ref"
title="Figure 5 ‣ 4.2 Conflicting gradients in practice ‣ 4 Benefits of MDL/MTL under the lens of model capacity ‣ Scalarization for Multi-Task and Multi-Domain Learning at Scale">Figure
<span class="ltx_ref"><span
class="ltx_text ltx_ref_tag">5</span></span>(a)</a>. On the one hand,
hyperparameters directly related to weights updates, such as the
learning rate of batch size, have a clear impact. In particular, since
memory consumption is often a bottleneck of gradient-based MTO methods,
this means that naively decreasing batch size to reduce memory usage may
severely impact such methods in practice. On the other hand, model
capacity has a lesser effect on the pattern of gradient conflicts,
despite influencing task interference as highlighted in
<a href="#S4.F2" class="ltx_ref"
title="Figure 2 ‣ 4 Benefits of MDL/MTL under the lens of model capacity ‣ Scalarization for Multi-Task and Multi-Domain Learning at Scale">Figure
<span class="ltx_ref"><span
class="ltx_text ltx_ref_tag">2</span></span></a>. Nevertheless, we also
observe that the overall magnitude of conflicts encountered for
different pairs of tasks reveal interesting task affinity patterns, as
illustrated in <a href="#S4.F5" class="ltx_ref"
title="Figure 5 ‣ 4.2 Conflicting gradients in practice ‣ 4 Benefits of MDL/MTL under the lens of model capacity ‣ Scalarization for Multi-Task and Multi-Domain Learning at Scale">Figure
<span class="ltx_ref"><span
class="ltx_text ltx_ref_tag">5</span></span>(b)</a>: For instance, the
<span id="S4.SS2.p3.1.1"
class="ltx_text ltx_font_typewriter">quickdraw</span> domain of
DomainNet appears as a clear outlier (across all model capacities),
which is also the case in terms of MDL performance in
<a href="#S4.F2" class="ltx_ref"
title="Figure 2 ‣ 4 Benefits of MDL/MTL under the lens of model capacity ‣ Scalarization for Multi-Task and Multi-Domain Learning at Scale">Figure
<span class="ltx_ref"><span
class="ltx_text ltx_ref_tag">2</span></span></a>.

</div>

class="ltx_text ltx_font_medium">We measure the variance across learning
rates while keeping other parameters fixed (model capacity, batch size,
etc.), then report the median variance across all settings. We repeat
this process for each axis of variation.</span></span></p>

class="ltx_text ltx_font_medium"> Proportion of pairwise task conflicts

<figcaption><span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_figure">Figure 5: </span>(<span
id="S4.F5.9" class="ltx_text ltx_font_italic">left</span>) Variance of
observed gradient conflicts when sweeping over different training
hyperparameters (high impact) as well as model capacity (low impact),
and (<span id="S4.F5.10" class="ltx_text ltx_font_italic">right</span>)
illustrating the median proportion of pairwise gradient conflicts as a
measure of task affinity</figcaption>
</figure>

<div id="S4.SS2.p4" class="ltx_para">

In summary, we observe intriguing properties of gradient conflicts in
practice, in particular suggesting that the extra cost of measuring,
storing and correcting conflicting gradients at every training iteration
can be superfluous. As an
alternative, \[<a href="#bib.bib30" class="ltx_ref">30</a>\] shows that
less costly regularization methods (e.g., early stopping) can be
competitive with MTO. In this work, we consider an orthogonal direction,
by tuning the scalarization/resampling weights hyperparameters to
regulate the training speed of the different tasks/domains. However, the
computational cost of browsing this large search space becomes a
practical caveat as $`T`$ grows. In the next section, we leverage a
scalable hyperparameter tuning algorithm to tackle this problem and
apply it on the DomainNet and CelebA datasets.

</div>

</div>

</div>

<div id="S5" class="section ltx_section">

## <span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_section">5 </span>Population-based training for scalarization weights selection

<div id="S5.p1" class="ltx_para">

Tuning the weights <span id="S5.p1.1.1"
class="ltx_text ltx_markedasmath ltx_font_bold">p</span> with classical
parameter search methods, such as grid search or random search, becomes
extremely costly when $`T`$ increases. To address this high
computational demand, we propose to leverage the population-based
training (PBT) \[<a href="#bib.bib24" class="ltx_ref">24</a>\] framework
which has been used for efficient hyperparameter search in reinforcement
learning \[<a href="#bib.bib24" class="ltx_ref">24</a>\] and for data
augmentation pipelines \[<a href="#bib.bib23" class="ltx_ref">23</a>\].

</div>

<div id="S5.SS0.SSS0.Px1" class="section ltx_paragraph">

#### Population-based training (PBT).

<div id="S5.SS0.SSS0.Px1.p1" class="ltx_para">

PBT is an evolutionary algorithm for hyperparameter search: $`N`$ models
are trained in parallel with different starting hyperparameters. Every
$`E_{ready}`$ epochs, the models synchronize: The $`Q\%`$ worst models
in the population are stopped, and their model weights and
hyperparameters are replaced by the ones of the $`Q\%`$ best models
(<span id="S5.SS0.SSS0.Px1.p1.1.1"
class="ltx_text ltx_font_italic">exploit step</span>); Then, the newly
copied hyperparameters are randomly perturbed to reach a new part of the
hyperparameter search space (<span id="S5.SS0.SSS0.Px1.p1.1.2"
class="ltx_text ltx_font_italic">explore step</span>). Finally, training
resumes until $`E`$ epochs are reached. In other words, PBT enables
dynamic exploration of the hyperparameter search space with a fixed
computational cost (cost of training $`N`$ models + potential overhead
from synchronization). A follow-up work, Population-based Bandits
(PB2) \[<a href="#bib.bib46" class="ltx_ref">46</a>\] proposes to
leverage Bayesian
optimisation \[<a href="#bib.bib17" class="ltx_ref">17</a>\] to better
guide the explore step. In contrast to PBT, PB2 also offers theoretical
guarantees on the convergence rate.

</div>

</div>

<div id="S5.SS0.SSS0.Px2" class="section ltx_paragraph">

#### Using PBT to tune scalarization weights.

<div id="S5.SS0.SSS0.Px2.p1" class="ltx_para">

PBT tuning relies on three important characteristics that may conflict
with the standard scalarization MDL/MTL training pipeline:

- <span id="S5.I1.i1"><span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_item">•</span></span>
  <div id="S5.I1.i1.p1" class="ltx_para">

  Models are trained with a dynamic schedule of hyperparameters. While
  this contrasts with standard scalarization in which $`\mathbf{p}`$ is
  fixed, we do not expect this to be an issue as recent work has shown
  that scalarization with dynamic random weights performs well in
  practice \[<a href="#bib.bib35" class="ltx_ref">35</a>\].

  </div>
- <span id="S5.I1.i2"><span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_item">•</span></span>
  <div id="S5.I1.i2.p1" class="ltx_para">

  Secondly, models are compared against one another after a few epochs
  of training ($`E_{ready}`$ epochs) in contrast to e.g. random search
  where models are usually trained until convergence, or reaching a
  certain stopping criterion. Consequently, tuning $`E_{ready}`$ can
  significantly impact the search’s stability and outcome, which we
  further discuss in <a href="#S5a" class="ltx_ref"
  title="5 PBT results ‣ Scalarization for Multi-Task and Multi-Domain Learning at Scale">Appendix
  <span class="ltx_ref"><span
  class="ltx_text ltx_ref_tag">5</span></span></a>.

  </div>
- <span id="S5.I1.i3"><span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_item">•</span></span>
  <div id="S5.I1.i3.p1" class="ltx_para">

  Finally, models in the population are compared using a single
  objective (e.g. average task/domain metric) during training. This may
  be an issue if the task metrics’ have widely different ranges and the
  PBT scheduler may simply learn to favor short term improvement by
  giving higher weight to tasks with high metrics; While we do not
  observe this issue in our settings, recent
  work \[<a href="#bib.bib15" class="ltx_ref">15</a>\] also proposes a
  multi-objective variant of PBT which may be better fitted for MDL/MTL
  applications.

  </div>

Nevertheless, the key advantage of PBT is its computational efficiency
with regard to search space exploration: For a constant cost of training
$`N`$ models, and some minor overhead related to checkpointing, PBT
explores up to $`N(1+Q\times E_{total}/E_{ready})`$ possible
hyperparameter configurations throughout training of the population.

</div>

<figcaption><span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_table">Table 1: </span>Results
of MDL when jointly training all 6 domains of DomainNet for
scalarization (uniform and PBT-found weights) and MTO methods. PBT is
run with a population size of
<math display="inline" xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><semantics><mrow><mi>N</mi><mo>=</mo><mn>12</mn></mrow><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">N=12</annotation></semantics></math>
models, such that every
<math display="inline" xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><semantics><mrow><msub><mi>E</mi><mrow><mi>r</mi><mi>e</mi><mi>a</mi><mi>d</mi><mi>y</mi></mrow></msub><mo>=</mo><mn>5</mn></mrow><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">E_{ready}=5</annotation></semantics></math>
epochs,
<math display="inline" xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><semantics><mrow><mi>Q</mi><mo>=</mo><mn>25</mn><mi>%</mi></mrow><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">Q=25\%</annotation></semantics></math>
of the population triggers an exploit/explore step.</figcaption>
</figure>

<div id="S5.SS0.SSS0.Px2.p2" class="ltx_para">

In <a href="#S5.T1" class="ltx_ref"
title="Table 1 ‣ Using PBT to tune scalarization weights. ‣ 5 Population-based training for scalarization weights selection ‣ Scalarization for Multi-Task and Multi-Domain Learning at Scale">Table <span
class="ltx_ref"><span class="ltx_text ltx_ref_tag">1</span></span></a>
and <a href="#S5.T2" class="ltx_ref"
title="Table 2 ‣ Using PBT to tune scalarization weights. ‣ 5 Population-based training for scalarization weights selection ‣ Scalarization for Multi-Task and Multi-Domain Learning at Scale">Table <span
class="ltx_ref"><span class="ltx_text ltx_ref_tag">2</span></span></a>,
we report results for searching optimal scalarization weights
$`\mathbf{p}^{\ast}`$ when training for all 6 domains of DomainNet and
for 8 tasks (attribute subsets) of CelebA. For PBT results, we first run
the search algorithm using the implementation from
Raytune \[<a href="#bib.bib33" class="ltx_ref">33</a>\]. We use 70% of
the training set for training, and use the remaining 30% to rank models
in the population by measuring their average accuracy on this set. Once
the search is done, we retrain a model on the full training set using
the scalarization weights found by PBT. For comparison, we also report
results for uniform scalarization and MTO methods, using the
implementation from  \[<a href="#bib.bib31" class="ltx_ref">31</a>\].
All final models are trained for three different learning rate and the
best metric is reported, averaged across 2 random seeds.

</div>

<div id="S5.SS0.SSS0.Px2.p3" class="ltx_para">

On the DomainNet example, we observe that the scalarization weights
found by PBT outperforms all methods, confirming our insights that
tuning weights $`\mathbf{p}`$ can further enhance scalarization. Note
that MTO methods were not designed or employed for multi-domain settings
such as DomainNet, which may explain why gradient-based MTO methods all
underperform their loss-based counterparts in the results of
<a href="#S5.T1" class="ltx_ref"
title="Table 1 ‣ Using PBT to tune scalarization weights. ‣ 5 Population-based training for scalarization weights selection ‣ Scalarization for Multi-Task and Multi-Domain Learning at Scale">Table <span
class="ltx_ref"><span class="ltx_text ltx_ref_tag">1</span></span></a>,
while they do exhibit good performance on CelebA MTL. In fact, on
CelebA, while PB2 search reaches the highest overall average metric, we
find that results across tasks exhibit more variance, with PB2 and
CAGrad yielding the best, comparable, performance. Overall, the very
narrow differences in accuracy makes it difficult to highlight one
specific method, which also raises the issue on whether CelebA is a
robust enough MTL benchmark. Nevertheless, both experiments show that
scalarization can outperform more complex optimization methods when its
weights are tuned properly, which can be done efficiently using scalable
hyperparameter search methods like PBT or PB2. We discuss further
insights and compute details in <a href="#S5a" class="ltx_ref"
title="5 PBT results ‣ Scalarization for Multi-Task and Multi-Domain Learning at Scale">Appendix
<span class="ltx_ref"><span
class="ltx_text ltx_ref_tag">5</span></span></a>.

</div>

<figcaption><span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_table">Table 2: </span>Results
of MTL when training on all 8 tasks (subset of attributes) of CelebA
defined in <a href="#S1a" class="ltx_ref"
title="1 Experimental settings and training hyperparameters ‣ Scalarization for Multi-Task and Multi-Domain Learning at Scale">Appendix
<span class="ltx_ref"><span
class="ltx_text ltx_ref_tag">1</span></span></a>. For PBT and PB2 we use
slightly different parameters than DomainNet to account for the fact
that CelebA contains more tasks, and hence has a larger search space:
All PBT runs use a population size of
<math display="inline" xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><semantics><mrow><mi>N</mi><mo>=</mo><mn>12</mn></mrow><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">N=12</annotation></semantics></math>
models, such that every
<math display="inline" xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><semantics><mrow><msub><mi>E</mi><mrow><mi>r</mi><mi>e</mi><mi>a</mi><mi>d</mi><mi>y</mi></mrow></msub><mo>=</mo><mn>3</mn></mrow><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">E_{ready}=3</annotation></semantics></math>
epochs,
<math display="inline" xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><semantics><mrow><mi>Q</mi><mo>=</mo><mn>40</mn><mi>%</mi></mrow><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">Q=40\%</annotation></semantics></math>
of the population triggers an exploit/explore step. For PB2 runs we use
a population size of
<math display="inline" xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><semantics><mrow><mi>N</mi><mo>=</mo><mn>8</mn></mrow><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">N=8</annotation></semantics></math>
and otherwise the same
<math display="inline" xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><semantics><mi>Q</mi><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">Q</annotation></semantics></math>
and
<math display="inline" xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><semantics><msub><mi>E</mi><mrow><mi>r</mi><mi>e</mi><mi>a</mi><mi>d</mi><mi>y</mi></mrow></msub><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">E_{ready}</annotation></semantics></math>
hyperparameters. For the sake of space, we omit standard deviations in
CelebA in the main text (in the range
<math display="inline" xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><semantics><mrow><mn>1</mn><msup><mi>e</mi><mrow><mi>−</mi><mn>4</mn></mrow></msup></mrow><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">1e^{-4}</annotation></semantics></math>),
and only report results for the four best performing MTO
baselines.</figcaption>
</figure>

</div>

</div>

<div id="S6" class="section ltx_section">

## <span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_section">6 </span>Limitations

<div id="S6.p1" class="ltx_para">

Since we are building off linear scalarization, our study suffers from
the same issue: It has been shown, for instance in
 \[<a href="#bib.bib5" class="ltx_ref">5</a>\], that scalarization only
finds solutions on the convex parts of the Pareto front. Same as
previous studies \[<a href="#bib.bib63" class="ltx_ref">63</a>,
<a href="#bib.bib52" class="ltx_ref">52</a>\], we do not observe this to
be an issue in practice, but there may be some cases where scalarization
can simply not perform as well as more advanced MTO methods. In
addition, our results and observations relies on an experimental study.
While we do attempt to experiment over a diverse set of benchmarks and
model capacities to get as many data points as possible, we can not
guarantee the generality of our claims across all MDL/MTL settings.

</div>

</div>

<div id="S7" class="section ltx_section">

## <span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_section">7 </span>Conclusions

<div id="S7.p1" class="ltx_para">

This work presents a comprehensive evaluation of scalarization’s
effectiveness in multi-domain and multi-task learning, spanning diverse
model capacities and dataset sizes. Our analysis reveals that
larger-capacity models often benefit more from joint learning across
diverse settings. In addition, tuning scalarization weights is key to
reach optimal performance at inference and improve the MTL/MDL model
generalization. Nevertheless,, given a specific set of specific
tasks/domains, the optimal weights are rather robust to changes in model
capacities within the same architecture family. We then investigate the
impact of model capacity on gradient conflicts observed during training
and observe low correlation with MTL/MDL performance. Finally, to tackle
the large search space of tuning scalarization weights, we propose to
leverage population-based training as a scalable, efficient method for
tuning scalarization weights as the number of tasks/domains increases.

</div>

</div>

  44(7):3614–3633, 2021. </span></span>

  Investigating and improving multi-task optimization in massively

  *International Conference on Learning Representations (ICLR)*, 2021.
  </span></span>

  </span> <span class="ltx_bibblock">In *Conference on Neural

- <span id="bib.bib64"><span class="ltx_tag ltx_role_refnum ltx_tag_bibitem">\[64\]</span>

<div class="ltx_pagination ltx_role_newpage">

</div>

<div id="p1" class="ltx_para">

<span id="p1.1.1" class="ltx_text" style="font-size:144%;">Supplemental
Material to “Scalarization for Multi-Task and Multi-Domain Learning at
Scale”</span>

</div>

<div id="S1a" class="section ltx_section">

## <span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_section">1 </span>Experimental settings and training hyperparameters

<div id="S1.SS1" class="section ltx_subsection">

### <span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_subsection">1.1 </span>Multi-Domain

<div id="S1.SS1.p1" class="ltx_para">

We refer to benchmarks as "multi-domain" when they contain multiple
input visual domains with a shared set of output classes (i.e.,
$`\forall i\neq j,X_{i}\neq X_{j}`$ and $`Y_{i}=Y_{j}`$).

</div>

<div id="S1.SS1.SSS0.Px1" class="section ltx_paragraph">

#### CIFAR-10 and STL-10.

<div id="S1.SS1.SSS0.Px1.p1" class="ltx_para">

CIFAR-10 \[<a href="#bib.bib29" class="ltx_ref">29</a>\] is a classical
benchmark for image classification containing 50k training samples
uniformly distributed across 10 classes.
STL-10 \[<a href="#bib.bib9" class="ltx_ref">9</a>\] is a
semi-supervised dataset which was designed to resemble CIFAR-10.
Specifically, we only use the 5000 annotated images in STL-10, which are
also uniformly distributed across the same 10 classes as CIFAR. In
STL-10, the images themselves are from the
ImageNet \[<a href="#bib.bib54" class="ltx_ref">54</a>\] dataset, and
cropped/resized to 96 pixels. We further resize them to 32 pixels to
align with CIFAR. In summary, the key difficulties are (i) the input
distribution shift between the two datasets and (ii) the high imbalance
in data availability.

</div>

<div id="S1.SS1.SSS0.Px1.p2" class="ltx_para">

We use a vision transformer backbone (ViT-S) optimized for small-scale
datasets \[<a href="#bib.bib18" class="ltx_ref">18</a>\] (compared to
the original ViT-S, this backbone contains smaller patch sizes, fewer
transformer layers and narrower embeddings, but a higher number of
heads). To control model capacity, we vary the depth (number of
transformer layers) in $`\{3,6,9\}`$ and the width (token dimension) in
$`\{48,96,144,192\}`$, Finally, we train each model from scratch on a
single NvidiaV100 GPU with a batch size of 256 images for 300 epochs
(including $`30`$ epochs of linear learning rate warmup), using a
learning rate of $`0.001`$ and weight decay of $`0.05`$ with the AdamW
optimizer and cosine learning rate decay.

</div>

</div>

<div id="S1.SS1.SSS0.Px2" class="section ltx_paragraph">

#### DomainNet.

<div id="S1.SS1.SSS0.Px2.p1" class="ltx_para">

DomainNet \[<a href="#bib.bib47" class="ltx_ref">47</a>\] is a
classification dataset of 6 visual domains annotated for 345 classes,
for a total of roughly 410k training samples. DomainNet was initially
introduced for the problem of multi-source domain adaption, in which one
or more of the domains does not have training annotations; the key
difficulty is thus to learn representations that are aligned across
domains. In contrast to the CIFAR+STL example, DomainNet exhibits
distribution shifts across both the input domains and output classes, as
visualized in <a href="#S1.F1" class="ltx_ref"
title="Figure A ‣ DomainNet. ‣ 1.1 Multi-Domain ‣ 1 Experimental settings and training hyperparameters ‣ Scalarization for Multi-Task and Multi-Domain Learning at Scale">Figure
<span class="ltx_ref"><span
class="ltx_text ltx_ref_tag">A</span></span></a>.

</div>

<figure id="S1.F1" class="ltx_figure">

<figcaption><span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_figure">Figure A:
</span>Illustrating the data imbalance in DomainNet with a contour plot
of the number of samples per class and domains in DomainNet. Each of the
corners of the hexagon represents one of the six domains in DomainNet,
and the lines (levels of the contour plot) represent the number of
samples, drawn every 15 classes. For comparison, a uniformly distributed
dataset would yield perfect hexagons.</figcaption>
</figure>

<div id="S1.SS1.SSS0.Px2.p2" class="ltx_para">

Following previous
literature \[<a href="#bib.bib47" class="ltx_ref">47</a>,
<a href="#bib.bib32" class="ltx_ref">32</a>\], we use a ResNet-101 as
our main backbone. There is no domain-dependent layer in the
architecture: the final classifier layer is shared across all domains.
We first perform a training sweep over the largest domain
(<span id="S1.SS1.SSS0.Px2.p2.1.1"
class="ltx_text ltx_font_typewriter">real</span>) to select the
best-performing learning rate from $`\{0.3,0.03,0.003,0.0003\}`$ and the
number of epochs from $`\{30,60,90\}`$. Following these results, we use
a learning rate of $`0.03`$ and train for 30 epochs with a batch size of
512 in subsequent experiments. We train with the AdamW optimizer with a
weight decay of $`1e-4`$. We also apply linear learning rate warm-up
during the first five training epochs and use cosine schedule learning
rate decay for the rest of the training. Finally, to control model
capacity, we vary the depth (backbone) in {ResNet-26, ResNet-50,
ResNet-101} and the width factor.

</div>

</div>

<div id="S1.SS1.SSS0.Px3" class="section ltx_paragraph">

#### Multi-domain, resampling and training length

<div id="S1.SS1.SSS0.Px3.p1" class="ltx_para">

In the multi-domain setting, scalarization weights become resampling
probabilities for each dataset, as shown in
(<a href="#S3.E2" class="ltx_ref"
title="In 3.1 Notations ‣ 3 Motivation and experimental setting for MTL/MDL ‣ Scalarization for Multi-Task and Multi-Domain Learning at Scale"><span
class="ltx_text ltx_ref_tag">2</span></a>). Consequently, the notion of
"epoch" is hard to define compare to the standard mono-dataset setting.
To resolve this, we always define epochs with respect to one of the
domains. For instance, in the CIFAR+STL case, we use STL as our
reference. Therefore, "one epoch" translates to seeing as many samples
as in the original STL dataset (5000) using the current batch size
(256), i.e. roughly 20 training steps. In the DomainNet case, we define
epochs relatively to the <span id="S1.SS1.SSS0.Px3.p1.1.1"
class="ltx_text ltx_font_typewriter">real</span> domain. This definition
has the advantage of not being impacted by the sampling weights
$`\mathbf{p}`$; In particular, this means that both the MDL models and
the single dataset (SD) baselines are trained for as many training
steps, and see the same amount of training samples, only sampled from
different data distributions.

</div>

</div>

</div>

<div id="S1.SS2" class="section ltx_subsection">

### <span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_subsection">1.2 </span>Multi-task

<div id="S1.SS2.p1" class="ltx_para">

We define multi-task benchmarks as datasets where every image is fully
annotated for multiple output tasks (i.e.,
$`\forall i\neq j,X_{i}=X_{j}`$ and $`Y_{i}\neq Y_{j}`$). This setting
is particularly popular for scene understanding problems where every
scene is labelled with multiple dense predictions (e.g. depth, normals,
segmentation mas, edges, etc.)

</div>

<div id="S1.SS2.SSS0.Px1" class="section ltx_paragraph">

#### Celeba.

<div id="S1.SS2.SSS0.Px1.p1" class="ltx_para">

CelebA is a binary attribute classification dataset containing 40
attributes and roughly 162k training images. To turn CelebA into a
multi-task problem, it is common to consider each attribute as a binary
classification task: More specifically, we use a fully shared backbone
with a final linear layer of 40 outputs, outputting logits for every
task. The model is then trained using 40 binary cross-entropy losses,
one for each attribute. To make our comparative analysis more scalable,
we define several tasks as subsets of attributes, grouped based on
semantic similarity (e.g. all hair colors are in the same subgroup). The
8 resulting subsets of attributes are described in
<a href="#S1.T1" class="ltx_ref"
title="Table A ‣ Celeba. ‣ 1.2 Multi-task ‣ 1 Experimental settings and training hyperparameters ‣ Scalarization for Multi-Task and Multi-Domain Learning at Scale">Table
<span class="ltx_ref"><span
class="ltx_text ltx_ref_tag">A</span></span></a>. In the scalarization
setting, this simply means that some of the attributes share the same
importance weight.

</div>

<div id="S1.SS2.SSS0.Px1.p2" class="ltx_para">

As a backbone, we use the same ViT-S/4 based architecture as for
CIFAR/STL. We train for 50 epochs with 5 epochs of learning rate warmup.
We use a learning rate of $`0.0005`$ with cosine schedule decay anf
train with the AdamW optimizer with a weight decay of $`0.05`$. We use
input images of size 32 (with tokens of size 4), a batch size of 256,
and RandAugment data augmentations.

</div>

<figcaption><span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_table">Table A: </span>The
eight tasks defined as subsets from CelebA attributes used in our main
analysis. Attributes in the same subset share a common importance weight
<math display="inline" xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><semantics><mi>p</mi><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">p</annotation></semantics></math></figcaption>
</figure>

</div>

<div id="S1.SS2.SSS0.Px2" class="section ltx_paragraph">

#### Taskonomy.

<div id="S1.SS2.SSS0.Px2.p1" class="ltx_para">

Taskonomy \[<a href="#bib.bib65" class="ltx_ref">65</a>\] is a large
dataset containing a variety of dense prediction tasks for indoor
scenes. We use the <span id="S1.SS2.SSS0.Px2.p1.1.1"
class="ltx_text ltx_font_typewriter">tiny</span> split of Taskonomy
which contains roughly 275k images. Taskonomy was originally introduced
for the problem of task clustering: The original
work \[<a href="#bib.bib65" class="ltx_ref">65</a>\] proposes a task
affinity metric to define a taxonomy of tasks. This taxonomy structure
is then used to determine which tasks should be trained from scratch and
which tasks could benefit from others via transfer learning. Closer to
our setting, follow-up works
 \[<a href="#bib.bib58" class="ltx_ref">58</a>,
<a href="#bib.bib16" class="ltx_ref">16</a>\] propose to use this
taxonomy to determine which tasks should be grouped or not in multi-task
learning. Once the groupings are determined, a separate backbone is
trained for each group of tasks. Instead, for our analysis, we use
Taskonomy-tiny in a more standard multi-task framework, where a backbone
is shared across tasks.

</div>

<div id="S1.SS2.SSS0.Px2.p2" class="ltx_para">

For training, we follow the methodology of
\[<a href="#bib.bib58" class="ltx_ref">58</a>\]. We use a ResNet-26
backbone (with varying bottleneck width) with a mirrored decoder; By
default, only the encoder is shared across tasks and each task receives
its own decoder. To vary model capacity, we add the option to share more
or fewer layers of the decoders across tasks. We use the same learning
rate of 0.1 and training for 100 epochs using a batch size of 256. We
train with SGD with a momentum of 0.9 and a weight decay of $`1e-4`$.
Following \[<a href="#bib.bib58" class="ltx_ref">58</a>\], all output
prediction maps are rescaled to have zero mean and unit variance on the
training set, and all dense tasks are trained with L1 loss.

</div>

</div>

</div>

</div>

<div id="S2a" class="section ltx_section">

## <span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_section">2 </span>Additional analysis results

<div id="S2.SS1" class="section ltx_subsection">

### <span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_subsection">2.1 </span>Complete results and methodology for <a href="#S4.F2" class="ltx_ref"
title="Figure 2 ‣ 4 Benefits of MDL/MTL under the lens of model capacity ‣ Scalarization for Multi-Task and Multi-Domain Learning at Scale">Figure
<span class="ltx_ref"><span
class="ltx_text ltx_ref_tag">2</span></span></a>

<div id="S2.SS1.p1" class="ltx_para">

In <a href="#S4" class="ltx_ref"
title="4 Benefits of MDL/MTL under the lens of model capacity ‣ Scalarization for Multi-Task and Multi-Domain Learning at Scale">Section
<span class="ltx_ref"><span
class="ltx_text ltx_ref_tag">4</span></span></a>, we perform MDL and MTL
experiments on several pairs of datasets, each time comparing to the
single dataset (SD) baseline trained for the same model capacity and
training length. All results are run for three random seeds on CelebA
and CIFAR+STL, and two random seeds for DomainNet and Taskonomy. To
present these results in a condensed form in
<a href="#S4.F2" class="ltx_ref"
title="Figure 2 ‣ 4 Benefits of MDL/MTL under the lens of model capacity ‣ Scalarization for Multi-Task and Multi-Domain Learning at Scale">Figure
<span class="ltx_ref"><span
class="ltx_text ltx_ref_tag">2</span></span></a>, we first find the
scalarization weights
$`\mathbf{p}^{\ast}=(p_{1}^{\ast},1-p_{1}^{\ast})`$ that yield the best
average accuracy across both datasets. Then we report the difference in
metrics between MDL trained with weights $`\mathbf{p}^{\ast}`$ and the
corresponding SD baseline, for each dataset. Note that for the Taskonomy
case, where the tasks are evaluated via L1 loss, we measure the negative
difference instead to keep the same interpretation as the other settings
where a positive value means MDL improves over SD.

</div>

<div id="S2.SS1.p2" class="ltx_para">

For completeness, we report all results for the CIFAR/STL case as
trade-off plots (accuracy on dataset 1 versus accuracy on dataset 2) in
<a href="#S2.F2" class="ltx_ref"
title="Figure B ‣ 2.1 Complete results and methodology for Figure ‣ 2 Additional analysis results ‣ Scalarization for Multi-Task and Multi-Domain Learning at Scale">Figure
<span class="ltx_ref"><span
class="ltx_text ltx_ref_tag">B</span></span></a> (CIFAR/STL) and in in
<a href="#S2.F3" class="ltx_ref"
title="Figure C ‣ 2.1 Complete results and methodology for Figure ‣ 2 Additional analysis results ‣ Scalarization for Multi-Task and Multi-Domain Learning at Scale">Figure
<span class="ltx_ref"><span
class="ltx_text ltx_ref_tag">C</span></span></a> (segmentation 2D and
depth tasks of Taskonomy). We observe the same trends as summarized in
the main paper: First, when increasing model capacity, the MDL
performance over the SD baseline increases; This is best seen when width
increases (across columns). Second, the optimal weights vary across
model sizes: At low width, the best performance is reached for a ratio
in the range of $`[0.3,0.4]`$. While larger models prefer
$`p^{\ast}_{\text{STL}}\in[0.1,0.3]`$. Finally, it is interesting to
note that these weights also differ from heuristics commonly used to set
scalarization weights: such as uniform scalarization
$`p_{\text{STL}}=0.5`$ or for instance setting the weights to match the
number of samples in each dataset $`p_{\text{STL}}=0.09`$. This further
highlights the fact that tuning scalarization weights can make
scalarization into a stronger baseline for MDL/MTL.

</div>

<figure id="S2.F2" class="ltx_figure">
<img src="2310.08910v1/cifar10_stl10_acc_acc_plot_test.png"
id="S2.F2.g1" class="ltx_graphics ltx_centering ltx_img_landscape"

alt="Refer to caption" />

(SD) is drawn in black and corresponds to the accuracy obtained when

circle markers correspond to the MDL model trained for different
scalarization weights
<math display="inline" xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><semantics><mi mathvariant="bold-italic">𝒑</mi><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">\mathbf{p}</annotation></semantics></math>.
The value of
<math display="inline" xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><semantics><msub><mi>p</mi><mtext mathvariant="normal">STL</mtext></msub><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">p_{\text{STL}}</annotation></semantics></math>
is represented as the color of the marker, while the remaining weight is
always set to
<math display="inline" xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><semantics><mrow><msub><mi>p</mi><mtext mathvariant="normal">CIFAR</mtext></msub><mo>=</mo><mn>1</mn><mo>−</mo><msub><mi>p</mi><mtext mathvariant="normal">STL</mtext></msub></mrow><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">p_{\text{CIFAR}}=1-p_{\text{STL}}</annotation></semantics></math>.</figcaption>
</figure>

The value of
<math display="inline" xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><semantics><msub><mi>p</mi><mtext mathvariant="normal">segmentation2D</mtext></msub><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">p_{\text{segmentation2D}}</annotation></semantics></math>
is represented as the color of the marker.</figcaption>
