</div>

<div id="S2.SS2" class="section ltx_subsection">

### <span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_subsection">2.2 </span>MDL helps generalization

<div id="S2.SS2.p1" class="ltx_para">

When comparing the MDL/MTL and SD performance, we often observe that
MDL/MTL improvements over SD are visible at inference but not at
training time (as illustrated for instance in
<a href="#S2.F4" class="ltx_ref"
title="Figure D ‣ 2.2 MDL helps generalization ‣ 2 Additional analysis results ‣ Scalarization for Multi-Task and Multi-Domain Learning at Scale">Figure
<span class="ltx_ref"><span
class="ltx_text ltx_ref_tag">D</span></span></a>). This indicates that
MDL/MTL training helps generalization of the model compared to training
on a single dataset. In particular, in the MDL setting, this draws an
interesting parallel with data augmentations: In fact, MDL training can
be seen as adding additional input data from a new distribution, with a
probability given by the scalarization weights $`\mathbf{p}`$, while
sharing the same semantic classes. And similarly to data augmentations,
adding this extra data source makes the training distribution harder to
fit (hence the SD baseline outperforming MDL at train time) but can
greatly benefit generalization performance (hence the inverse trend at
inference).

</div>

<div id="S2.SS2.p2" class="ltx_para">

To push the analogy further, the experimental study of
\[<a href="#bib.bib41" class="ltx_ref">41</a>\] suggests that a good
data augmentation should be one with a high <span id="S2.SS2.p2.1.1"
class="ltx_text ltx_font_italic">affinity</span> to the original data
distribution (i.e., the distribution shift between the original data and
augmented one should not be too significant) as well as a high
<span id="S2.SS2.p2.1.2"
class="ltx_text ltx_font_italic">diversity</span> (i.e., the added data
should be complex enough, which can be measure e.g. with magnitude of
the training loss). This hypothesis also matches our observations: For
instance adding <span id="S2.SS2.p2.1.3"
class="ltx_text ltx_font_typewriter">infograph</span> data to the
<span id="S2.SS2.p2.1.4"
class="ltx_text ltx_font_typewriter">real</span> domain leads to a low
affinity pairing but with high diversity and yields weaker MDL
performance on the <span id="S2.SS2.p2.1.5"
class="ltx_text ltx_font_typewriter">real</span> images compared to the
SD baseline (see <a href="#S4.F2" class="ltx_ref"
title="Figure 2 ‣ 4 Benefits of MDL/MTL under the lens of model capacity ‣ Scalarization for Multi-Task and Multi-Domain Learning at Scale">Figure
<span class="ltx_ref"><span
class="ltx_text ltx_ref_tag">2</span></span></a>).

</div>

<div id="S2.SS2.p3" class="ltx_para">

Finally, we note that the problem of finding optimal scalarization
weights mirrors the one of finding data augmentation hyperparameters,
which has been extensively explored for the task of image
classification. It has led to now commonly-adopted augmentation
strategies such as
RandAugment \[<a href="#bib.bib12" class="ltx_ref">12</a>\],
AutoAugment \[<a href="#bib.bib11" class="ltx_ref">11</a>\], or
PBA \[<a href="#bib.bib23" class="ltx_ref">23</a>\], which also uses
Population-based training to tackle this problem.

</div>

<figure id="S2.F4" class="ltx_figure">
<div class="ltx_flex_figure">
<div class="ltx_flex_cell ltx_flex_size_1">
<img src="2310.08910v1/x1.png" id="S2.F4.g1"
class="ltx_graphics ltx_centering ltx_figure_panel ltx_img_landscape"
style="aspect-ratio:476/176;" width="476" height="176"
alt="Refer to caption" />
</div>
<div class="ltx_flex_break">

</div>
<div class="ltx_flex_cell ltx_flex_size_1">
<img src="2310.08910v1/cifar10_stl10_acc_acc_plot_train.png"
id="S2.F4.g2"
class="ltx_graphics ltx_centering ltx_figure_panel ltx_img_landscape"
style="aspect-ratio:476/176;" width="476" height="176"
alt="Refer to caption" />
</div>
</div>
<figcaption><span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_figure">Figure D:
</span>Train-test Discrepancy when comparing MDL/MTL improvement over
the SD baseline visualized on the CIFAR+STL example. In particular, in
the MDL setting, this matches the classical interpretation of data
augmentations: Adding additional semantically relevant data from an
input distribution may be harder to fit at training time but leads to
improved generalization performance at inference.</figcaption>
</figure>

</div>

</div>

<div id="S3a" class="section ltx_section">

## <span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_section">3 </span>Methodology for measuring gradients conflicts

<div id="S3a.p1" class="ltx_para">

In this appendix, we briefly describe our methodology for
<a href="#S4.SS2" class="ltx_ref"
title="4.2 Conflicting gradients in practice ‣ 4 Benefits of MDL/MTL under the lens of model capacity ‣ Scalarization for Multi-Task and Multi-Domain Learning at Scale">Section
<span class="ltx_ref"><span
class="ltx_text ltx_ref_tag">4.2</span></span></a>. We use the same
definition of gradient conflicts as in
PCGrad \[<a href="#bib.bib64" class="ltx_ref">64</a>\]: Two task/domain
specific gradients are conflicting if and only if the cosine of the
angle between them is strictly negative. We train a model using standard
uniform scalarization, measure the number of conflicting pairs of
task/domain gradients over one epoch of training, and report it as a
percentage (of all pairs), for each epoch during training.

</div>

<div id="S3a.p2" class="ltx_para">

We report these results in the main text in
<a href="#S4.F4" class="ltx_ref"
title="Figure 4 ‣ 4.2 Conflicting gradients in practice ‣ 4 Benefits of MDL/MTL under the lens of model capacity ‣ Scalarization for Multi-Task and Multi-Domain Learning at Scale">Figure
<span class="ltx_ref"><span
class="ltx_text ltx_ref_tag">4</span></span></a> and in
<a href="#S3.F5" class="ltx_ref"
title="Figure E ‣ 3 Methodology for measuring gradients conflicts ‣ Scalarization for Multi-Task and Multi-Domain Learning at Scale">Figure
<span class="ltx_ref"><span
class="ltx_text ltx_ref_tag">E</span></span></a> below. Our main
observation is that the presence or absence of gradient conflicts does
not correlate well with actual MDL/MTL performance throughout trainined.
This challenges the assumption underlying many multi-task optimization
(MTO) methods \[<a href="#bib.bib64" class="ltx_ref">64</a>,
<a href="#bib.bib38" class="ltx_ref">38</a>,
<a href="#bib.bib62" class="ltx_ref">62</a>\] that reducing gradient
conflicts leads to improved MTL performance. This also aligns well with
recent results of \[<a href="#bib.bib63" class="ltx_ref">63</a>,
<a href="#bib.bib30" class="ltx_ref">30</a>\] showing that MTO methods
that reduce gradient conflicts do not outperform simpler scalarization
approaches in practice, and with the hypothesis of
\[<a href="#bib.bib30" class="ltx_ref">30</a>\] that many MTO methods
can be reinterpreted as regularization techniques.

</div>

<figure id="S3.F5" class="ltx_figure">
<div class="ltx_flex_figure">
<div class="ltx_flex_cell ltx_flex_size_1">

</div>
<div class="ltx_flex_break">

</div>
<div class="ltx_flex_cell ltx_flex_size_1">
<p><span id="S3.F5.2.1" class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold">(a)</span>
DomainNet. Individual curves are colored by batch size.</p>
</div>
</div>
<figcaption><span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_figure">Figure E:
</span>Additional result on measuring conflicting gradients when
training with standard uniform scalarization for DomainNet (6
domains).</figcaption>
</figure>

</div>

<div id="S4a" class="section ltx_section">

## <span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_section">4 </span>Consistency of optimal scalarization weights

<div id="S4a.p1" class="ltx_para">

As noted in the analysis from <a href="#S4" class="ltx_ref"
title="4 Benefits of MDL/MTL under the lens of model capacity ‣ Scalarization for Multi-Task and Multi-Domain Learning at Scale">Section
<span class="ltx_ref"><span
class="ltx_text ltx_ref_tag">4</span></span></a>, the optimal weights
$`\mathbf{p}^{\ast}`$ is rather consistent across model depths and
widths. For instance, on the CIFAR/STL case, $`\mathbf{p}^{\ast}`$
always falls in the range of \[0.2, 0.4\].

</div>

<div id="S4a.p2" class="ltx_para">

This can also be seen from the qualitative results of the
population-based training search of scalarization weights in
<a href="#S5.SS2" class="ltx_ref"
title="5.2 Qualitative results ‣ 5 PBT results ‣ Scalarization for Multi-Task and Multi-Domain Learning at Scale">Section
<span class="ltx_ref"><span
class="ltx_text ltx_ref_tag">5.2</span></span></a>: While the history of
hyperparameter changes during the search differ, PBT tends to converge
to similar distribution for the scalarization weights $`\mathbf{p}`$
across different model depths and widths. This suggests that the
theoretical search space for $`\mathbf{p}`$ may be reduced in practice
leading to a more computationally efficient search: Performing a rough
initial search on a smaller model from the same architecture family can
provide a promising range for $`\mathbf{p}`$, and can then be refined by
searching with the larger target architecture.

</div>

</div>

<div id="S5a" class="section ltx_section">

## <span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_section">5 </span>PBT results

<div id="S5a.p1" class="ltx_para">

Because the search space for scalarization weights $`\mathbf{p}`$ grows
exponentially with the number of tasks, classical hyperparameter search
methods such as grid search or random search would struggle to scale as
the number of tasks increases. Bayesian optimization
(BO) \[<a href="#bib.bib17" class="ltx_ref">17</a>\] allows for faster
results by browsing the search space in a smart way by building and
following a probabilistic model of the hyperparameters. However, BO
still requires training models to convergence (or until an early
stopping criterion is met) which can be computationally expensive.
Instead, we experiment with the Population-based Training framework
\[<a href="#bib.bib24" class="ltx_ref">24</a>\] for searching for the
optimal scalarization weights. PBT relies on the assumption that the
"goodness" of a certain hyperparameter choice can be evaluated in a few
epochs during training, rather than having to finish a full run of
training.

</div>

<div id="S5.SS1" class="section ltx_subsection">

### <span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_subsection">5.1 </span>Compute resources

<div id="S5.SS1.p1" class="ltx_para">

As mentioned in the main paper we perform all training runs on a single
NVIDIA V100 machine with 32GB of memory. However, both the PBT and MTO
models require higher compute resources than a single normal run of
training, which we discuss below.

</div>

<div id="S5.SS1.p2" class="ltx_para">

- <span id="S5.I1.i1a"><span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_item">•</span></span>
  <div id="S5.I1.i1a.p1" class="ltx_para">

  <span id="S5.I1.i1a.p1.1.1" class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold">PBT</span>
  requires training a population of <span id="S5.I1.i1a.p1.1.2"
  class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold">N</span> models that are regularly
  synchronized; then followed by a final training run with the found
  optimal weights. Following previous
  works \[<a href="#bib.bib23" class="ltx_ref">23</a>\], we perform the
  hyperparameter search on a smaller subset of the data (in our
  experiments $`r=0.7`$ fraction of the training set). In summary, the
  expected computational cost is roughly $`Nr+1`$ times higher than
  standard training. On the CelebA example, we also observe that using
  PB2 \[<a href="#bib.bib46" class="ltx_ref">46</a>\], which combines
  the benefits of Bayesian Optimisation and PBT, yields better
  hyperparameters using a smaller population size. In terms of memory
  usage, PBT is the same as a standard training run: Synchronization is
  handled via checkpoints saving and loading, such that only one model
  lives in memory. Finally, we use the publicly available PBT
  implementation from
  Raytune\[<a href="#bib.bib33" class="ltx_ref">33</a>\] which handles
  all synchronization operations across the population. The
  implementation would also scale well to more compute resources, as the
  Ray API allows for easy parallelization.

  </div>
- <span id="S5.I1.i2a"><span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_item">•</span></span>
  <div id="S5.I1.i2a.p1" class="ltx_para">

  <span id="S5.I1.i2a.p1.1.1" class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold">MTO</span>.
  As shown in <a href="#S3.F1" class="ltx_ref"
  title="Figure 1 ‣ 3.2 Motivation ‣ 3 Motivation and experimental setting for MTL/MDL ‣ Scalarization for Multi-Task and Multi-Domain Learning at Scale">Figure
  <span class="ltx_ref"><span
  class="ltx_text ltx_ref_tag">1</span></span></a>, the bottleneck in
  most gradient-based methods is memory usage. Consequently, this
  requires us to decrease the batch size to meet memory requirements and
  compensate with gradient accumulation (or parallelism if multiple
  devices are available). For instance, on the DomainNet experiments
  with all 6 domains, we need to decrease from a batch size of 512 to a
  batch size of 128 with 4 steps of accumulation to still fit in memory
  requirements. This also raises the question of how to handle
  synchronization across batches: For instance, in PCGrad, the gradient
  conflicts (and projections) can be computed either
  <span id="S5.I1.i2a.p1.1.2" class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold">(i)</span>
  per local batch, before accumulation: this may lead to noisier
  updates; or <span id="S5.I1.i2a.p1.1.3"
  class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold">(ii)</span> after gradients are
  accumulated: However this is more memory-intensive as this requires to
  store the previously accumulated per-task gradient as well as the one
  being currently computed. In practice we use the implementation of
  \[<a href="#bib.bib31" class="ltx_ref">31</a>\] for all MTO methods.

  </div>

</div>

<div id="S5.SS1.p3" class="ltx_para">

When comparing different hyperparameter searches for scalarization, PBT
allows for much faster exploration of the search space than classical
techniques such as grid search. However, comparing PBT+scalarization
with MTO is less straightforward as the computational cost depends on
many factors (e.g. population size, number of tasks, and impact on
memory usage, etc.), but generally, the "scalarization + hyperparameter
search" approach is more favorable in case of low memory requirements as
it does not change memory costs compared to standard training. However,
soTA gradient-based methods are not very costly for a low number of
tasks (e.g. 2-3) as shown in <a href="#S3.F1" class="ltx_ref"
title="Figure 1 ‣ 3.2 Motivation ‣ 3 Motivation and experimental setting for MTL/MDL ‣ Scalarization for Multi-Task and Multi-Domain Learning at Scale">Table
<span class="ltx_ref"><span
class="ltx_text ltx_ref_tag">1</span></span></a> which makes them
appealing in settings with a few tasks. Nevertheless, one of our key
takeaways is that allocating extra resources for tuning scalarization
weights, to mirror the extra resources needed for MTO training, makes
scalarization into a much stronger baseline, on-par or even
outperforming MTO methods as shown in <a href="#S5" class="ltx_ref"
title="5 Population-based training for scalarization weights selection ‣ Scalarization for Multi-Task and Multi-Domain Learning at Scale">Section
<span class="ltx_ref"><span
class="ltx_text ltx_ref_tag">5</span></span></a>. Finally, another
important difference is that hyperparameter search methods directly
optimize for the target objective: the optimal hyperparameters are found
by maximizing the average task/domain accuracy on a hold-out validation
dataset. In contrast, MTO methods optimize for a proxy metric (such as
reducing gradients conflicts) that may not always correlate with final
performance as shown in <a href="#S4.SS2" class="ltx_ref"
title="4.2 Conflicting gradients in practice ‣ 4 Benefits of MDL/MTL under the lens of model capacity ‣ Scalarization for Multi-Task and Multi-Domain Learning at Scale">Section
<span class="ltx_ref"><span
class="ltx_text ltx_ref_tag">4.2</span></span></a>.

</div>

</div>

<div id="S5.SS2" class="section ltx_subsection">

### <span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_subsection">5.2 </span>Qualitative results

<div id="S5.SS2.p1" class="ltx_para">

In this section, we report some qualitative results of the
hyperparameter scheduled found by PBT and PB2. At the end of PBT search,
we select the model with the highest validation performance and
backtrack its history to backtrack its choices of hyperparameter values
during training: This yields the policy of optimal weights found by PBT
which is then used to retrain a model on the full training set. We also
experimented with retraining a model using the last weights of the
policy, but this usually slightly underperform using the whole history
of weights in the majority of cases.

</div>

<div id="S5.SS2.p2" class="ltx_para">

In <a href="#S5.F6" class="ltx_ref"
title="Figure F ‣ 5.2 Qualitative results ‣ 5 PBT results ‣ Scalarization for Multi-Task and Multi-Domain Learning at Scale">Figure
<span class="ltx_ref"><span
class="ltx_text ltx_ref_tag">F</span></span></a>, we report examples of
the policy of weights found by PBT and PB2 search for the parameters
$`E_{ready}=3`$, $`Q=40\%`$ and $`N=12`$ for PBT (respectively $`N=8`$
for PB2).

</div>

<figure id="S5.F6" class="ltx_figure">
<div class="ltx_flex_figure">
<div class="ltx_flex_cell ltx_flex_size_2">
<div id="S5.F6.2"
class="ltx_block ltx_figure_panel ltx_minipage ltx_align_center ltx_align_middle"
style="width:155.3pt;">
<p>width = 1</p>
</div>
</div>
<div class="ltx_flex_cell ltx_flex_size_2">
<div id="S5.F6.3"
class="ltx_block ltx_figure_panel ltx_minipage ltx_align_center ltx_align_middle"
style="width:155.3pt;">
<p>width = 0.25</p>
</div>
</div>
<div class="ltx_flex_break">

</div>
<div class="ltx_flex_cell ltx_flex_size_1">
<p><span id="S5.F6.4.1" class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold">(a)</span>
Comparing Population-based training results for a depth of 9 layers with
full width (left) and a smaller model with a quarter of the width
(right). While both policy are quite different across training epochs,
they converge towards similar distribution: For instance the weights for
tasks "hair style", "gender" and "age" are significantly smaller than
the one for the "mouth" and "hair style" tasks.</p>
</div>
<div class="ltx_flex_break">

</div>
<div class="ltx_flex_cell ltx_flex_size_1">
<div id="S5.F6.5"
class="ltx_block ltx_figure_panel ltx_minipage ltx_align_center ltx_align_middle"
style="width:155.3pt;">
<p>depth = 9</p>
</div>
</div>
<div class="ltx_flex_break">

</div>
<div class="ltx_flex_cell ltx_flex_size_1">
<div id="S5.F6.6"
class="ltx_block ltx_figure_panel ltx_minipage ltx_align_center ltx_align_middle"
style="width:155.3pt;">
<p>depth = 6</p>
</div>
</div>
<div class="ltx_flex_break">

</div>
<div class="ltx_flex_cell ltx_flex_size_1">
<p><span id="S5.F6.7.1" class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold">(b)</span>
Comparing Population-based training results for a depth of 9 layers with
full width (left) and a smaller model with a depth of 6 layers (right).
Similarly to the results on varying width in <span id="S5.F6.7.2"
class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold">(a)</span>, both search converge to
similar distribution in task weights</p>
</div>
<div class="ltx_flex_break">

</div>
<div class="ltx_flex_cell ltx_flex_size_1">
<div id="S5.F6.8"
class="ltx_block ltx_figure_panel ltx_minipage ltx_align_center ltx_align_middle"
style="width:155.3pt;">
<p>Population-based Training (PBT)</p>
</div>
</div>
<div class="ltx_flex_break">

</div>
<div class="ltx_flex_cell ltx_flex_size_1">
<div id="S5.F6.9"
class="ltx_block ltx_figure_panel ltx_minipage ltx_align_center ltx_align_middle"
style="width:155.3pt;">
<p>Population-based Bandit (PB2)</p>
</div>
</div>
<div class="ltx_flex_break">

</div>
<div class="ltx_flex_cell ltx_flex_size_1">
<p><span id="S5.F6.10.1" class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold">(c)</span>
Comparing Population-based training results for a depth of 9 layers with
full width (left) and the same search with Population-based bandit
(right). The two search algorithms converge to significantly different
results in particular regarding weights for the "mouth" and "facial
hair" tasks. This suggests that (i) there may be multiple good local
minima in the search space of <span id="S5.F6.10.2"
class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold">p</span> and (ii) the heuristic used in
the explore step has a significant impact on how the resulting
policy.</p>
</div>
</div>
<figcaption><span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_figure">Figure F:
</span>Qualitative results for Population-based training search on
CelebA. The x-axis represents training epochs. The y-axis represents the
policy scalarization weights for each task as a cumulative histogram for
the run of the population with highest validation accuracy</figcaption>
</figure>

</div>

<div id="S5.SS3" class="section ltx_subsection">

### <span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_subsection">5.3 </span>Quantitative results

<div id="S5.SS3.p1" class="ltx_para">

Here we report additional results for the CelebA and DomainNet
experiments of <a href="#S5" class="ltx_ref"
title="5 Population-based training for scalarization weights selection ‣ Scalarization for Multi-Task and Multi-Domain Learning at Scale">Section
<span class="ltx_ref"><span
class="ltx_text ltx_ref_tag">5</span></span></a>, in the style of
<a href="#S5.T1" class="ltx_ref"
title="Table 1 ‣ Using PBT to tune scalarization weights. ‣ 5 Population-based training for scalarization weights selection ‣ Scalarization for Multi-Task and Multi-Domain Learning at Scale">Table
<span class="ltx_ref"><span
class="ltx_text ltx_ref_tag">1</span></span></a> and
<a href="#S5.T2" class="ltx_ref"
title="Table 2 ‣ Using PBT to tune scalarization weights. ‣ 5 Population-based training for scalarization weights selection ‣ Scalarization for Multi-Task and Multi-Domain Learning at Scale">Table
<span class="ltx_ref"><span
class="ltx_text ltx_ref_tag">2</span></span></a> in the main text: We
include results using a ResNet-50 on DomainNet in
<a href="#S5.T2a" class="ltx_ref"
title="Table B ‣ 5.3 Quantitative results ‣ 5 PBT results ‣ Scalarization for Multi-Task and Multi-Domain Learning at Scale">Table
<span class="ltx_ref"><span
class="ltx_text ltx_ref_tag">B</span></span></a>, and results for
additional widths in CelebA in <a href="#S5.T3" class="ltx_ref"
title="Table C ‣ 5.3 Quantitative results ‣ 5 PBT results ‣ Scalarization for Multi-Task and Multi-Domain Learning at Scale">Table
<span class="ltx_ref"><span
class="ltx_text ltx_ref_tag">C</span></span></a> and
<a href="#S5.T4" class="ltx_ref"
title="Table D ‣ 5.3 Quantitative results ‣ 5 PBT results ‣ Scalarization for Multi-Task and Multi-Domain Learning at Scale">Table
<span class="ltx_ref"><span
class="ltx_text ltx_ref_tag">D</span></span></a>.

</div>

<figcaption><span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_table">Table B: </span>Results
of MDL when jointly training on all 6 domains of DomainNet for
scalarization (uniform and PBT-found weights) and MTO methods with a
<span id="S5.T2a.3" class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold">ResNet50</span>
backbone. PBT is run with a population size of
<math display="inline" xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><semantics><mrow><mi>N</mi><mo>=</mo><mn>12</mn></mrow><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">N=12</annotation></semantics></math>
models, such that every
<math display="inline" xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><semantics><mrow><msub><mi>E</mi><mrow><mi>r</mi><mi>e</mi><mi>a</mi><mi>d</mi><mi>y</mi></mrow></msub><mo>=</mo><mn>5</mn></mrow><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">E_{ready}=5</annotation></semantics></math>
epochs,
<math display="inline" xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><semantics><mrow><mi>Q</mi><mo>=</mo><mn>25</mn><mi>%</mi></mrow><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">Q=25\%</annotation></semantics></math>
of the population triggers an exploit/explore.</figcaption>
</figure>

<figcaption><span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_table">Table C: </span>(Table
best seen zoomed in PDF) Results of MTL when training on all 8 tasks
(subset of attributes) of CelebA for a depth of 6 layers For PBT and PB2
we use slightly different parameters than DomainNet to account for the
fact that CelebA contains more tasks, and hence has a larger search
space: All PBT runs use a population size of
<math display="inline" xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><semantics><mrow><mi>N</mi><mo>=</mo><mn>12</mn></mrow><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">N=12</annotation></semantics></math>
models, such that every
<math display="inline" xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><semantics><mrow><msub><mi>E</mi><mrow><mi>r</mi><mi>e</mi><mi>a</mi><mi>d</mi><mi>y</mi></mrow></msub><mo>=</mo><mn>3</mn></mrow><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">E_{ready}=3</annotation></semantics></math>
epochs,
<math display="inline" xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><semantics><mrow><mi>Q</mi><mo>=</mo><mn>40</mn><mi>%</mi></mrow><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">Q=40\%</annotation></semantics></math>
of the population triggers an exploit/explore step. For PB2 runs we use
a population size of
<math display="inline" xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><semantics><mrow><mi>N</mi><mo>=</mo><mn>8</mn></mrow><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">N=8</annotation></semantics></math>
and otherwise the same
<math display="inline" xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><semantics><mi>Q</mi><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">Q</annotation></semantics></math>
and
<math display="inline" xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><semantics><msub><mi>E</mi><mrow><mi>r</mi><mi>e</mi><mi>a</mi><mi>d</mi><mi>y</mi></mrow></msub><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">E_{ready}</annotation></semantics></math>
hyperparameters.</figcaption>
</figure>

<figcaption><span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_table">Table D: </span>(Table
best seen zoomed in PDF) Results of MTL when training on all 8 tasks
(subset of attributes) of CelebA for a depth of 9 layers. For PBT and
PB2 we use slightly different parameters than DomainNet to account for
the fact that CelebA contains more tasks, and hence has a larger search
space: All PBT runs use a population size of
<math display="inline" xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><semantics><mrow><mi>N</mi><mo>=</mo><mn>12</mn></mrow><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">N=12</annotation></semantics></math>
models, such that every
<math display="inline" xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><semantics><mrow><msub><mi>E</mi><mrow><mi>r</mi><mi>e</mi><mi>a</mi><mi>d</mi><mi>y</mi></mrow></msub><mo>=</mo><mn>3</mn></mrow><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">E_{ready}=3</annotation></semantics></math>
epochs,
<math display="inline" xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><semantics><mrow><mi>Q</mi><mo>=</mo><mn>40</mn><mi>%</mi></mrow><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">Q=40\%</annotation></semantics></math>
of the population triggers an exploit/explore step. For PB2 runs we use
a population size of
<math display="inline" xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><semantics><mrow><mi>N</mi><mo>=</mo><mn>8</mn></mrow><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">N=8</annotation></semantics></math>
and otherwise the same
<math display="inline" xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><semantics><mi>Q</mi><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">Q</annotation></semantics></math>
and
<math display="inline" xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><semantics><msub><mi>E</mi><mrow><mi>r</mi><mi>e</mi><mi>a</mi><mi>d</mi><mi>y</mi></mrow></msub><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">E_{ready}</annotation></semantics></math>
hyperparameters.</figcaption>
</figure>
